An honest key set always passes: the transcript check disqualifies a seat whose signing key repeats another qualified seat's or any identity key (`duplicate-key`), so this only keeps one key from signing receipts for two seats, or an identity key from signing as a per-election key, on the board ([[trustee-identity]], Which key signs what; T-37).
9. **The approvals' seats**: strictly increasing, the first at least 1 and the last at most `n`, so each seat counts once. ECDSA lets one key make many valid signatures on one statement, so counting signatures instead of seats would let one seat, or one stolen key, reach a quorum alone ([[trustee-identity]], Ceremony registration; T-14, T-37). An approving seat needn't be in the key set: a seat the check disqualified can still have checked the transcript.
10. **The signatures**: each approval's signature verifies, under its seat's pinned key, on the ceremony registration statement ([[trustee-identity]], Ceremony registration) with `l2_chain_id = block.chainid`, `trustee_registry` this contract, the record's `panel_id`, the approval's `index`, `stub_hash`, `ceremony_transcript_hash`, and `key_set_hash` computed from the record's seats. The registry builds `key_set_hash` and the 157-byte statement and its digest `H(DS("abolish/v1/sig/ceremony-registration-2", m))` itself, and verifies through OpenZeppelin's `P256.verify` after checking `1 ≤ r < n` and `1 ≤ s ≤ (n − 1) / 2` itself ([[trustee-identity]], On chain). Every approval must verify, including any beyond the quorum, so no record holds a signature the panel didn't make.

It then records, for (`stub_hash`, `panel_id`), `ceremony_transcript_hash`, `key_set_hash`, the key set's seats and each one's signing key, and emits `CeremonyRegistered`. Nothing recorded can be changed or removed, and a second registration for the same stub and panel reverts, with the same transcript or another: a quorum can't hold a second set of keys in reserve for a definition ([[ceremony]], Approval and re-runs). The sender is never checked or recorded (T-71).

**The registered key set** is read with `keySetOf(stub_hash, panel_id)`, which returns `key_set_hash` and the seats as a bitmask (bit `i` set for seat `i`, so bit 0 never), and `signingKeyOf(stub_hash, panel_id, index)`, which returns seat `index`'s signing key as `(x, y)`, both zero for a seat outside the set or a stub and panel with nothing recorded. The board takes an election's qualified seats and its trustees' signing keys from these, under the stub hash and panel its definition gives, never from a submitter ([[0028-board-contract-checks]], 2B; Board, Ballots, step 8). They are what a quorum signed, not what the transcript check returns: [[verifier]] 3.1 compares the two and fails an election whose recorded set differs, so a set a corrupt quorum signs makes the board accept the wrong keys only until that election fails (Known-weak).

**Per stub and panel.** A stub names one panel, and an honest seat signs only for a stub that names its own ([[ceremony]], Stub checks). Recording per stub alone would let a quorum of another panel the deployment pins register first and block the stub's own panel for good (T-25); keyed by panel, its record is simply never asked for, since the election registry passes the definition's own panel.

`ceremonyRecorded(stub_hash, transcript_hash, panel_id, threshold, panel_size)` returns true exactly when `panel_id` is pinned with that threshold and size, and the transcript recorded for (`stub_hash`, `panel_id`) is non-zero and equals `transcript_hash`. `panel_id` already binds `k` and `n`; comparing them as well costs nothing and keeps the answer right whatever the caller passes.

### Author registration check

`checkAuthorRegistration(bytes registration, uint256[2][] keys, uint8 threshold)` is a view the election registry calls for an author panel's registration (Election registry, Author panel registration, checks 6 to 9). It checks `registration`'s length as Registration's check 2 does (`MalformedRegistration`), then its key set as checks 7 and 8 do, with `threshold` as `k` (`KeySetTooSmall`), `keys.length` as `n` (`KeySetSeatsOutOfOrder`) and `keys` as the panel's identity keys (`InvalidSigningKey`, `DuplicateSigningKey`), then its approvals as checks 9 and 10 do, each signature under `keys[index − 1]` on the statement naming this registry and the record's `panel_id`, `stub_hash`, `ceremony_transcript_hash` and the key set's hash (`SeatsOutOfOrder`, `InvalidSignature`), in the same order and with the same code as `registerCeremony`. It returns `key_set_hash` and the seats as a bitmask, as `keySetOf` would report them, and reverts otherwise.

It is a function of its arguments alone: it reads no pinned panel, checks neither the quorum nor the header against anything, and records nothing, so calling it changes no state and proves nothing to anyone but its caller. The election registry checks the header and the quorum against the definition before calling (checks 1 to 5) and passes the points of the definition's `author_keys`; anyone else who calls it learns only whether those seats signed that record. An author approval it accepts never counts in `registerCeremony`, whose panel must be pinned, and no author panel is (`panel_id` under the author tag, [[trustee-identity]]).

### Events

Each event goes through the event accumulator, with topic 0 the keccak-256 of its signature:

| Event                                                                                                                       | Topics                                              | Data                                                                |
| --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `PanelPinned(bytes32 indexed panelId, uint8 threshold, uint8 quorum, uint8 kind, bytes keys)`                               | `panel_id`                                          | `k`, `q`, the kind and the `33 · n` bytes of encodings, ABI-encoded |
| `CeremonyRegistered(bytes32 indexed stubHash, bytes32 indexed panelId, bytes32 indexed transcriptHash, bytes registration)` | `stub_hash`, `panel_id`, `ceremony_transcript_hash` | The whole registration record, ABI-encoded as `bytes`               |

The panels are logged at deployment, so they are the first events of the registry's sequence (which, until 1.2 binds creation code, only the release constructor guarantees: Board anchor, Known-weak, Constructor effects aren't bound). A verifier finds an election's ceremony registration by its stub and panel, checks every approval against the panel its release pins ([[trustee-identity]], Ceremony registration), and counts the distinct seats whose approvals verify against the quorum its release pins for that panel, never the `q` in `PanelPinned`, which it compares with its own and reports if they differ (P1-19, [[verifier]] 3.1); and 2.5 judges `panel-kind` by the kind its release pins, comparing the kind `PanelPinned` logs for the definition's panel with it and reporting a difference, as 3.1 does the quorum's (Deployment, The kind). The trustee registry logged `PanelPinned` without the kind until [[P2-16]] part 5e-2, as contract-event versions 1 to 11 frame it, and logs the kind since; version 12 frames the kind-carrying event only (Contract-event record, Event tables (version 12)). A verifier recomputes `key_set_hash` from the record's seats, which is the one every approval signs, and compares it with the hash of the key set its own transcript check returns ([[verifier]] 3.1). It needs nothing else from the contract's state. Like the election registry, the trustee registry is shared by every election, so its event sequence is replayed whole from the archive ([[verifier]] 4.4; Election registry, The shared event sequence). Only a quorum can add an event to it, so it grows with ceremonies, not with junk.

### Vectors

The Solidity tests consume `vectors/trustee.json`: the deployment check accepts every key vector `p256Key` accepts and refuses every other, at any length; every signature vector verifies exactly when it is marked valid; every panel vector is pinned with its `panel_id`, or refused with its code. `trustee.json`'s `registrations` are statements of the retired first layout and are read by nothing ([[trustee-identity]], Ceremony registration). The tests also consume `vectors/key-set.json`: every key set's `key_set_hash` is the one the registry computes from its seats' points, each point is the one its `p256-key` encodes, and every statement's message and digest are the ones the registry builds, with a signature that verifies only under its seat's key; registrations that carry the file's key sets record them, with their seats and signing keys, and the key-set checks (size, order, points, repeated and identity keys) each have a refusal test. They also consume `vectors/contract-event-v12.json`: deployed at its trustee registry's address and chain, at its records' block and time, with the panels its `PanelPinned` records log, the registry emits exactly those five records and its accumulator reaches the file's after them (the sequence's `CeremonyRegistered`, copied from version 2's vectors, names a panel none of the five is). Check 7 refuses kinds 0 and 3 to 255 with `kind`, after `quorum` and `duplicate-panel`, and a kind word above 255, whatever its low byte, never reaches it: the constructor's decoder refuses it.

### Known-weak

- **The precompile is the chain's code, not ours or OpenZeppelin's.** The contract is built for `cancun`, which has no `P256VERIFY`, so the tests' default run takes `P256.verify`'s Solidity fallback (about 250,000 gas a signature). The package's test script runs the trustee registry's tests again on `osaka`, where Foundry provides the precompile (EIP-7951), so both paths pass every vector; a 7-seat registration then costs about 570,000 gas with its key set (Gas, below). Base implements the precompile itself (RIP-7212), and a disagreement between its implementation and Foundry's would first show on a testnet deployment.
- **The vendored library is checked against itself.** `test/vendor.test.ts` pins the vendored files' hashes, which the package README records with their upstream tag and commit; re-fetching them from upstream in CI needs a `ci:` change (rule B).
- **A quorum can register a transcript for any stub**, including one no election will register. It costs gas, blocks nothing, and needs `q` seats' signatures, but each such event puts bytes the quorum chose into the shared archive every verifier replays: 64 for `stub_hash` and `ceremony_transcript_hash`, and about 64 per seat of its key set, since the registry can't check a `kem_key_hash` against any key and a signing key need only be an unused point (about half of all `x` values give one, so about 32 chosen bytes), up to `MAX_TRUSTEES` seats: about 1 KB per registration (T-17).
- **A key set a corrupt quorum signs is recorded.** The registry can't run the transcript check (it needs ML-KEM), so it records the set the quorum signed, and the board accepts receipts and complaints under its keys until [[verifier]] 3.1 fails the election for a set that isn't the transcript's. A quorum of `max(k, n − 1)` corrupt seats can already break the election's secrecy, so this adds a way to fail, not a new attack ([[0028-board-contract-checks]], Known-weak; T-14).
- **Gas.** In the tests, registering the 7-seat panel's transcript with all 7 seats in the key set and 4 approvals costs about 1.5 million gas under OpenZeppelin's Solidity fallback and about 570,000 through the precompile, against about 1.1 million and 130,000 for the same approvals without a key set: most of the difference is the 16 storage words of signing keys and the set. It runs once per election and panel.
- **The kind is the deployer's word.** The registry can't see who holds a seat, so it can't check that an `independent` panel meets [[0006-trustees]] (at most one seat ours): a deployer can log a transitional panel as `independent`, and the election registry then registers civic-record definitions under it. Each still fails [[verifier]] 2.5 (`panel-kind`) against the kind its release pins, and the ballot client refuses to cast in it, so a false kind can stall such polls, never put one on the civic record unflagged (T-14); logging an independent panel as `transitional` only refuses its civic polls. The kind is an input of the deployment, public in `PanelPinned`, so anyone can compare it with the release's deployment record; that the logged kind is the stored one, which `kindOf` returns, holds only for the release constructor until [[verifier]] 1.2 binds creation code (Board anchor, Known-weak, Constructor effects aren't bound).
- **The quorum is the deployer's choice.** `q` is a constructor input held in storage, so verifier 1.2's bytecode check doesn't pin it, and a deployer can pick any `q` from `k` to `n`, or pin extra panels. The `k` floor holds whatever it picks. A verifier must not inherit the choice: it counts the distinct valid seats in the `CeremonyRegistered` record itself (Events, above), against the quorum its release pins.

## Group registry

Threats: T-06 (group stuffing by the operator), T-03 (a second nullifier key, and so a second nullifier for every document), T-54 (a stalled key), T-62 (contract admin abuse), T-71 (trusting who sent a transaction), T-08 and T-13 (an electorate that isn't public before voting), T-31 (malleable signatures), T-17 (chosen bytes in the archive), T-52 (junk in a shared event sequence), T-12 (linking a member to a poll).

The group registry holds the deployment's Semaphore groups. It fixes each group's tier when it creates the group and logs every addition and removal together with the authorisation a verifier rechecks, so [[verifier]] 2.4 rebuilds each tier tree ([[membership]], Tier tree) from the chain and the board alone. It keeps no Merkle tree: the roots elections use are tier-tree roots that verifiers compute from these events, and direct submission checks a ballot's proof against the root registered with its definition ([[membership]], Owned elsewhere), so a root kept on chain would be a second answer nothing may use. It is immutable: no owner, proxy, pause or upgrade path, and it inherits the event accumulator above.

This version adds members to **Tier 0** groups, by the registrars' signatures, to **self-paid** Tier 0 groups, by anyone who pays the gas to add their own commitment ([[0024-tier0-registration]], option D; Self-paid additions), and to **Tier 1** groups, by anonymous vouches that anyone may submit ([[0014-tier1-vouching]], [[vouching]]). It also records the Tier 2 scope's keyed-nullifier keys, the initial key and its reshares (Nullifier keys). Tier 2 additions (document proofs, [[0010-tier2-identity-provider]], P3-4) will be specified here before any group registry but the staging deployment's is deployed (Testnet deployments), since a deployed registry's rules can't change; until then `createGroup` refuses tier 2. `packages/contracts` builds the Tier 1 call ([[P3-3]] part 3b-1), the self-paid calls ([[P3-2]] part 5b-1) and the two nullifier-key calls ([[P3-4]] part 2b-4b-3).

### Deployment

The registry is deployed with its **registrars**: `n` Ethereum addresses (secp256k1 keys) and a threshold `t`. [[0004-l2-choice]] (Governance as an adversary) has Tier 0 additions carry a threshold signature of our Safe's owners, checked by the contract and again by the verifier. This registry keeps that rule's purpose (no transaction spoofed from any address, by L2 governance or anyone else, adds members without `t` keys) with two departures, listed under Decisions to review in STATUS:

- **Pinned, not read from the Safe.** A Safe's owners, threshold and modules can change after deployment (Board anchor, Known-weak), and a verifier reading them would have to prove the Safe's state at every addition's block. Pinned, the set a verifier checks is the one the registry's first event logs and its release pins.
- **Dedicated keys, not the Safe's owner keys.** Tier 0 registration is automatic, so the signing service holds `t` registrar keys online (Known-weak). Registrar keys MUST be used for nothing else, never as owners of the Safe that holds the anchoring authority or any timelock, so that seizing the registration service seizes Tier 0 additions and nothing more (T-69, T-62). The deployment record names the registrars and who holds each key.

`t`, the cached EIP-712 domain separator and the chain ID it was computed for are immutables, which [[verifier]] 1.2 masks and compares with the values its release pins; the addresses are in storage, so the verifier checks them through `RegistrarsPinned` (Verification). Rotating the registrars means deploying a new registry, whose groups are new groups. Deployment reverts unless, in this order:

1. `2 ≤ n ≤ MAX_REGISTRARS = 16` (`size`);
2. `2 ≤ t ≤ n` (`threshold`): no single key adds or removes members (T-62);
3. no address is zero, and the addresses are strictly ascending as 160-bit integers (`order`), so none repeats and the set has one encoding;
4. the chain ID fits a `u64`, as for every contract;
5. the trustee registry it is given pins the initial panel (`initial-panel`; Nullifier keys, Deployment).

`RegistrarsPinned` logs `t` and the addresses, and the deployment record (`packages/contracts/deployments/<chain-id>.json`) carries them.

### Groups

`createGroup(uint8 tier)` takes calldata of exactly its 4-byte selector and one word holding the tier, and creates group `group_id`, the number of groups the registry created before it, counted from 0 (the `group_id` of [[election-definition]]), with that tier, fixed for good. It reverts unless the tier is 0 or 1 (`2` is refused until its addition path exists), and rather than let `group_id` leave the `u64` range. The registry keeps each group's tier, its member count (Additions) and the L2 block that created it, which the Tier 1 path reads (Tier 1 additions).

`createSelfPaidGroup()` takes calldata of exactly its 4-byte selector and creates group `group_id` from the same counter as a **self-paid** group of tier 0, fixed for good, reverting rather than let `group_id` leave the `u64` range. Every other group is a **signed** group. A group's kind is the event that created it: `GroupCreated` makes a signed group of the tier it logs, and `SelfPaidGroupCreated` a self-paid group of tier 0. Only self-paid additions enter a self-paid group, and no signed record may name one (Self-paid additions). [[0019-regional-cells]] requires each group's region to be logged at its creation before any registry but the staging deployment's is deployed (Testnet deployments); when `createGroup` gains the region, `createSelfPaidGroup` and `SelfPaidGroupCreated` gain it in the same change, and until then a self-paid group's region, like a signed group's, is the release's and the definition's word ([[registration]], Groups).

Anyone can create a group. A new Tier 0 group has no members until the registrars add some, a new self-paid group none until someone pays to add one, and a new Tier 1 group none until vouched-for people are added, each spending three Tier 2 members' slots, so creating one gives its sender nothing, and costs others only an event in the registry's shared sequence (Election registry, The shared event sequence; T-52). A group's tier is the one its `GroupCreated` event logs: the verifier takes it from there, never from a definition's label ([[election-definition]], A group's tier; [[verifier]] 2.4).

The identifier depends only on the counter, so an L2 reorganisation that reorders two creations swaps their identifiers. Every signed message binds the group's tier as well as its identifier (Signed messages, below), so a batch signed for one group can never land in a group of another tier; registrars MUST still sign for a group only once its `GroupCreated` event is in finalized L2 history, or a reorg could move a batch into a stranger's group of the same tier.

### Additions

`addMembers(bytes record)` takes one record:

```
record   = u64(group_id) ‖ u64(first_index) ‖ u16(count) ‖ field<bn254> × count ‖ u8(approvals) ‖ approval × approvals
approval = u8(seat) ‖ bytes[32](r) ‖ bytes[32](s) ‖ u8(v)
```

so the record is exactly `19 + 32 · count + 66 · approvals` bytes, with `approvals` the byte at offset `18 + 32 · count`. `r` and `s` are big-endian integers. Anyone may submit a record. The registry checks, in this order, and reverts on the first failure (the approvals are checked one by one, seat then signature, in record order):

1. **Canonical calldata**, as for the election registry's `registerDefinition` (T-17).
2. **Length**: at least 19 bytes, and exactly `19 + 32 · count + 66 · approvals`. Nothing can be appended (T-17).
3. **The group** was created by this registry as a signed group, and its tier is 0. A self-paid group never takes a signed batch (Self-paid additions, Why separate groups).
4. **The batch**: `1 ≤ count ≤ MAX_BATCH = 256`, which bounds the gas, the log and the accumulator step of one call.
5. **The position**: `first_index` equals the group's member count, the number of commitments added to it so far (removals don't lower it), and `first_index + count ≤ 2³²`, the leaves of a tier tree ([[membership]], Tier tree).
6. **The commitments**: none is forbidden ([[membership]], Commitments and removals). Each is a canonical `field<bn254>` (below `r`), and none is `0`, `1` or `Poseidon2(0, 1)`:

   ```
   Poseidon2(0, 1) = 0x1bd20834f5de9830c643778a2e88a3a1363c8b9ac083d36d75bf87c49953e65e
   ```

   (circomlib's Poseidon with two inputs, the hash of the tier tree; Vectors, below, says where the value comes from).

7. **Enough seats**: `approvals ≥ t`.
8. **The seats and signatures**: each approval's `seat` is greater than the previous one's, at least 1 and at most `n`, so each registrar counts once; its `v` is 27 or 28, `1 ≤ r < N` and `1 ≤ s ≤ (N − 1) / 2` with `N` secp256k1's group order (the low-s rule of EIP-2, so a signature has one encoding, T-31); and `ecrecover` of the `AddMembers` digest (Signed messages) under `(v, r, s)` returns registrar `seat`'s address. Every approval must verify, including any beyond `t`, so no record carries a signature the registrars didn't make.

It then adds `count` to the group's member count and emits `MembersAdded`. Member `first_index + i`, leaf `first_index + i` of the tier tree, is the record's `i`-th commitment.

**Why the position is signed.** Binding `first_index` makes each signed batch land at one place, at most once: a replay, or a batch submitted out of order, reverts. So the group's sequence is exactly the one the registrars signed, and no relay can inflate a group's size by replaying a batch (T-06, T-13). Two batches signed for one position can't both land; the one submitted second is dead, and its members must be signed again at a later position. So the signing service MUST sign at most one batch per position, submit its records itself, and sign the next position only once the previous batch is final; a record handed to anyone else could be withheld to stall the group's queue (liveness only).

A commitment may repeat, within a group or across groups ([[membership]], Commitments and removals): the registry can't refuse a repeat without storing every commitment, and a repeated commitment is one identity with two leaves, which adds no voting identity but counts twice in `group_size`. The signing path MUST refuse a commitment the group already holds, and the forbidden ones (above).

### Removals

`removeMembers(bytes record)` takes one record:

```
record = u64(group_id) ‖ u64(size) ‖ u16(count) ‖ u64(index) × count ‖ u8(approvals) ‖ approval × approvals
```

exactly `19 + 8 · count + 66 · approvals` bytes, with `approvals` the byte at offset `18 + 8 · count`. `size` is the group's member count when the registrars signed. The registry checks, in this order: canonical calldata; the length; the group, as for additions (it exists, it is a signed group and its tier is 0); `1 ≤ count ≤ MAX_BATCH`; `size` is at most the group's member count; index by index, that it is greater than the one before, below `size` and not removed already; then rules 7 and 8 of Additions over the `RemoveMembers` digest. It then marks each index removed and emits `MembersRemoved`. A removal sets that leaf of the tier tree to 0 ([[membership]], Commitments and removals); the member count doesn't change, so leaf numbering never shifts.

**Why `size` is signed.** Without it, a removal signed (by mistake or in a race) for an index no member has reached yet would stay valid for good: whoever holds it could submit it once a legitimate member lands there, removing that member at a moment of their choosing, just before an election's `root_l2_block`, say. With it, every index a signed removal names was a member's when it was signed. The signing path MUST set `size` to the member count in finalized L2 history and remove only members it has identified there.

[[membership]] lets a verifier meet stray removals (an index no addition has reached, a leaf already removed) and ignore them. This registry refuses them, so each of its removals changes exactly the leaves it names; a replayed removal reverts.

### Self-paid additions

Threats: T-25 (a registrant refused by our service or by Cloudflare), T-49 (our domain and hosting gone), T-16 (a voter moved between cells), T-52 (junk in the shared sequence), T-17, T-71, T-11 (the paying address).

The brief's direct-submit path at Tier 0 ([[0024-tier0-registration]], option D): a registrant whom the signing service or Cloudflare refuses, or who can't reach either, adds their own commitment and pays the gas, with no registrars' signature.

`addSelfPaidMember(bytes record)` takes one record:

```
record = u64(group_id) ‖ field<bn254>(commitment)
```

exactly 40 bytes, sent with exactly `SELF_PAID_FEE` wei. Anyone may submit one, and the registry never reads the sender's address (T-71). It checks, in this order, and reverts on the first failure:

1. **Canonical calldata**, as for `addMembers` (T-17).
2. **Length**: exactly 40 bytes.
3. **The fee**: `msg.value` equals `SELF_PAID_FEE`, a constant of the registry's bytecode (Values, below).
4. **The group** was created by this registry as a self-paid group.
5. **The position**: the group's member count is below 2³², the leaves of a tier tree.
6. **The commitment** isn't forbidden (Additions, rule 6).

It then adds 1 to the group's member count and emits `SelfPaidMemberAdded` with `index`, the member count before the call: member `index` of the group, leaf `index` of its tier tree, is the commitment.

**Values.** `SELF_PAID_FEE` is `2 · 10¹⁴` wei (0.0002 ETH). The registry has no function that moves ether out of it, no owner and no `selfdestruct`, so every fee stays locked in it for good: a burn that pays no one, us included, and so gives no one a reason to want floods. Every other function of the registry refuses ether. The fee prices what gas alone doesn't (Junk, below); a change is a new registry.

**Junk.** Unlike a Tier 1 addition that doesn't count, every self-paid addition is a counted leaf of the group's tier tree. So [[verifier]] 4.4 requires every election that lists the group to board each of its `SelfPaidMemberAdded` events up to the tier's `root_l2_block` (for a rolling tier, in every block whose timestamp is at or before `closes_at`: Election registry, The shared event sequence, Rolling tiers), and every ballot client of such an election to read every record of the group before casting ([[election-definition]], The electorate is in the past). Signed additions are bounded by the service's batches (at most `MAX_BATCH` per group and period, [[registration]]); self-paid ones only by what their sender pays. On gas alone (a few tens of thousands per call on an L2) a state actor could add millions to the published groups before an election's `root_l2_block` and stall its boarding, its verification and its clients (T-52, T-53). With the fee, a million additions cost 200 ETH beyond gas, while a registrant pays it once. The residual, and the operator's answer to a flood that is paid anyway (a new published self-paid group for the region, listed by later definitions, whose registrants must add themselves again), are in Known-weak.

**One commitment and no position.** The record names no position, so a self-paid addition never reverts because another landed first. One that bound its position, as a signed batch does, would let anyone kill a pending addition by landing any addition to the same group ahead of it, at the price of gas (T-25). The registry assigns the index and logs it, and a verifier checks the logged indices against its own count (Verification). One commitment per call is what a registrant needs; a batch would only make bulk additions cheaper.

**Why separate groups.** A self-paid addition to a signed group would move the member count that every signed batch in flight binds, and kill it (Additions, Why the position is signed), so anyone could stall Tier 0 registration for one cheap transaction per period. So self-paid additions go only into self-paid groups, which no signed record may name: `addMembers` and `removeMembers` refuse a self-paid group, and `addSelfPaidMember` refuses a signed one (`group`). Nothing a self-paid addition does can make a signed record revert.

**No duplicate check.** The registry refuses no commitment for being held already, in this group or any other. Refusing a commitment live anywhere in Tier 0 would let anyone self-add a commitment from a signed batch in flight and kill the batch (T-25). Refusing only self-paid additions of a held commitment would need a storage write per signed commitment too (a commitment-to-index map, about 22,000 gas each, so about 5.6 million for a full batch), and would buy nothing the verifier's ranking doesn't give: a copier who lands first keeps the place either way. Repeats are resolved by [[membership]]'s first-occurrence rule (Cell tree), under which every signed occurrence ranks above every self-paid one, and self-paid occurrences rank by chain order, so a self-paid copy of an earlier registration never displaces it (T-16; [[0024-tier0-registration]], Amendment 1).

**No removals.** `removeMembers` refuses a self-paid group, and nothing else removes from one. The only removal keys are the registrars', and this path exists so that joining Tier 0 doesn't rest on them: registrars who could remove self-paid members could undo every addition it makes (T-25, T-06). The cost: a lost or stolen self-paid secret stays a member for the registry's life, as at Tier 1, and so do a Sybil's self-paid commitments (T-01 is unchanged, since Tier 0 has no Sybil resistance beyond its cost). A removal from a signed group doesn't keep a commitment out of Tier 0 either: removed leaves are 0 before the first-occurrence rule runs, so anyone can self-add a removed commitment again, and it counts in any definition that lists the group (T-18; [[membership]], Cell tree).

**Front-running.** Base has no public mempool, so a transaction sent to the sequencer is seen pending only by the RPC endpoint it went through and by the sequencer. Two routes are public, though: a call sent through the L1 portal, the path for a registrant the sequencer censors (T-25), sits in L1's public mempool and in L1 blocks for minutes before L2 includes it; and an attempt that reverts (too little gas, the wrong fee) publishes its commitment on L2 before it is resent. On the first two routes the endpoint or the sequencer, and on the public ones anyone, can copy the commitment into a self-paid group of another region and land the copy first, which then ranks first and puts the registrant in the copier's cell (T-16; [[0024-tier0-registration]], option D, the first residual). The client sees where its commitment counts and can register again only under a new identity secret ([[registration]], The direct path).

**Who pays.** The sender pays the fee and the call's gas: one storage write, the accumulator step and the event, with no signature to recover: about 47,500 gas of execution in a transaction (56,500 measured from a calling contract, whose `CALL` adds 9,000 for the value), beside the transaction's 21,000 and about 1,000 of calldata ([[P3-2]] part 5b-1). The registration paymaster doesn't sponsor it (Registration paymaster, Sponsorship), and neither does the API. The paying address is on L2 next to the commitment, so how it was funded can link the registrant to it, as for a direct-submitted ballot (T-11, T-25); the client says so before sending ([[registration]], The direct path). The fee and gas are the Sybil cost here, which at Tier 0 changes nothing T-01 doesn't already allow ([[0024-tier0-registration]], option D, Cons). The direct path isn't batched, so an addition's block time is its registrant's registration time, to the second (T-13).

### Tier 1 additions

Threats: T-06 (Tier 1 stuffing), T-52 (junk in the shared sequence), T-25 and T-53 (an honest addition blocked or a definition failed by someone else's submission), T-22 (a held vouch linking two vouchees), T-17, T-71.

`addTier1Member(bytes record)` takes one Tier 1 addition record ([[vouching]], Tier 1 addition record), with `C_V` the vouchee's commitment and `k = VOUCH_K` vouches. Anyone may submit one: the vouchee, or a relay for them. Its authorisation is the vouches themselves, never a signature of the registrars or of the sender, and the registry never reads the sender's address (T-71). The registry checks, in this order, and reverts on the first failure:

1. **Canonical calldata**, as for `addMembers` (T-17).
2. **Length**: exactly `41 + 626 · VOUCH_K` bytes (1,919), with `VOUCH_K` at offset 40, checked before any other field is read, so a padded or short record costs nothing to refuse (T-52).
3. **The group**: `group_id` was created by this registry, and its tier is 1.
4. **The commitment**: `C_V` isn't forbidden ([[membership]], Commitments and removals).
5. **Each vouch's fields**, vouch by vouch in record order: `slot < VOUCH_SLOTS`; `root` isn't 0 and is below `r`; both nullifiers are below `r`; `root_block` is a multiple of `VOUCH_ROOT_INTERVAL`, and `root_block ≤ block.number ≤ root_block + VOUCH_ROOT_WINDOW`; and `tier2_group_id` is a group of this registry whose tier is 2, created in a block at or before `root_block`. `block.number` is the L2 block of the call, which is the `b_A` the event accumulator binds to its log ([[vouching]], Order).
6. **Distinct vouchers and slots**: the `k` vouchee nullifiers are pairwise distinct, and so are the `k` slot nullifiers.
7. **The proofs**: in record order, each vouch's vouchee proof and then its slot proof passes `MembershipProofLib` (Board, Membership proofs), with signals `[ρ, ν_V, m̂_V, ŝ_V]` and `[ρ, ν_S, m̂_S, ŝ_j]`. The registry computes every scope and message itself, from `block.chainid`, its own address, `group_id`, `C_V` and the vouch's own fields ([[vouching]], Scopes, Messages), never takes one from the submitter, and checks the proofs exactly as [[membership]] decodes and verifies them, so it and a verifier never disagree about a proof (T-31).

It then emits `Tier1MemberAdded` with the whole record. It stores nothing for the addition beyond the accumulator step: no member count, no leaf and no nullifier.

An election naming a Tier 1 group boards the group's `GroupCreated` and exactly its counted additions, one per leaf, and boarding one of its additions that doesn't count fails [[verifier]] 4.4; other groups' additions and the Tier 2 history Tier 1 counting reads stay in the archive, since boarding every junk addition on every such election is the cost the shared event sequence exists to avoid ([[verifier]], Contract events, Tier 1 boarding; Election registry, The shared event sequence; T-52).

**Only checks no other addition can change.** Every check above depends only on the record, the block of the call and the group table, which only `createGroup` and `createSelfPaidGroup` write. The registry leaves three of [[vouching]]'s deployment checks to the verifier, deliberately:

- **`root-mismatch`**: the registry keeps no tree, so it can't tell a vouch's root from a root of a tree its submitter made up. Anyone can therefore log an addition with valid proofs against a made-up root, for any `C_V`, with secrets of their own.
- **`slot-spent`** and **`member`**: because of that, the registry can't tell a counted addition from one that fails `root-mismatch`. A registry that refused a slot nullifier or a commitment some earlier logged addition carried would let anyone who sees an honest addition before it lands block it: copy one of its vouches into an addition for the same group and `C_V` whose other vouches name a made-up root, land that first, and the honest addition reverts, its vouches' slots shown spent in an addition that never counts (T-25, T-53). Without that state, the copy fails `root-mismatch`, consumes nothing, and the honest addition lands and counts.

So no addition, logged or pending, makes the registry refuse another one, and the verifier decides what counts from the board alone, repeating every check above itself ([[0014-tier1-vouching]], Rules for P3-2: contract checks are spam control).

**What the checks buy.** On the release's bytecode, on an honest chain, every logged addition passes every check of one addition ([[vouching]], Checks of one addition), `proof` included, and the deployment's `group`, `root-grid`, `root-window` and `tier2-group` checks. So an addition the verifier doesn't count fails `root-mismatch`, `slot-spent` or `member`, each decided before `proof`. A verifier that checks proofs last ([[vouching]], Batched verification, Proofs last) then spends pairings only on additions that count, which the Tier 2 members' slots bound, and never on a batch that falls back; junk costs it the record's decoding (six proofs' points, about thirteen hashes) and a root lookup or a set lookup, never a pairing (T-52). That holds for a verifier that checks proofs last: one that batches every proof of the replay first also checks the proofs of verbatim or re-randomised copies of counted additions, which anyone can log without bound (Known-weak). Junk still costs its sender the six proofs' on-chain checks each time. And a vouch held past its root window can't be logged, which keeps a stale vouch off the chain, though not out of its vouchee's hands ([[vouching]], Known-weak).

**No Tier 1 removals.** `removeMembers` refuses a Tier 1 group (`group`, as for every tier but 0), and nothing else removes a Tier 1 member. Whoever can remove one can exclude people, and the only removal keys are the registrars', so a Tier 1 removal path would make leaving Tier 1 rest on the registrars, the very party Tier 1's admission was built not to rest on (T-06, T-62). Re-admitting a removed commitment with new vouches would make removal moot anyway ([[0014-tier1-vouching]], Rules for P3-2, Removals; Amendment 2). The cost: a Tier 1 member who loses their secret, or has it stolen, stays a member under it. They can be vouched for again under a fresh commitment, but the thief keeps the old identity (Known-weak; T-69).

**Who pays.** The sender pays the call's gas: about 1.49 million consumed in the tests (the registry's execution and its event; a transaction adds 21,000 and about 31,000 of calldata), mostly the six membership checks, but each check needs about 620,000 available when its pairing runs (Board, Membership proofs), so a call needs a gas limit of about 1.79 million or it reverts (measured: the least limit a counted addition succeeds with, on cold state). The registration paymaster doesn't sponsor it (Registration paymaster, Sponsorship). The API relays additions for vouchees from its own account (P2-3), so a vouchee needs no gas or account. A call that only simulates successfully proves nothing that counts: junk naming made-up roots and copies of counted additions both pass, so the API MUST relay only an addition that counts under [[vouching]]'s every check against its own replay of the registry, `root-mismatch`, `slot-spent`, `member` and `proof` included, and MUST rate-limit relays as it does Tier 0 registrations, or anyone can spend its account's funds at about 1.5 million gas a request (T-52). [[tier1-relay]] specifies both. A vouchee whose addition the API refuses can submit it directly (T-25), paying from an address whose funding is then linked to `C_V` on chain, beyond the network address ([[vouching]], Known-weak; T-11, T-13).

### Nullifier keys

The registry records the Tier 2 scope's keyed-nullifier key ([[nullifier-key]], [[nullifier-reshare]]): its one initial key and the chain of reshares that hands it from panel to panel. Approvals are recorded here and nowhere else, so whether a key counts is decided on chain, once, by rules no seat's view of the chain can change on an honest L2 history (a rewritten one is the T-71 residual, Known-weak): the registry records **at most one initial key per scope, ever**, and a successor only of the key that is current, which recording it replaces, so the scope's keys form one chain from its initial key (T-03). The registry can't run a transcript check, which needs the transcripts and ML-KEM, so [[verifier]] 2.4 replays these records with the checks of each key's transcript added (Verification). This is [[P3-4]] part 2b-4b; the Tier 2 additions that use the current key come with its later steps, and until then `createGroup` still refuses tier 2.

**Deployment.** Besides its registrars, the registry is deployed with the address of the deployment's trustee registry and the `panel_id` of the scope's **initial panel**, the one the release pins for the initial key ([[nullifier-key]], Parties and keys). Both are immutables, which [[verifier]] 1.2 masks and compares with the values its release pins, readable as `trusteeRegistry()` and `initialPanelId()`. No event logs them: the registrars have `RegistrarsPinned` because they are in storage, which 1.2 doesn't read, while these two are in the code 1.2 already compares, and an event at deployment would put one that no contract-event version lists yet into every registry's sequence, which every reader of the sequence under an earlier version refuses (`unknown-event`; Events). After the registrars' four checks, deployment reverts unless the trustee registry pins the initial panel: its `panelOf(initial_panel_id)` gives a threshold that isn't zero (`initial-panel`, reverting with `InitialPanel`).

The registry reads every panel from the trustee registry: a panel's `k` and `n` from `panelOf`, and seat `i`'s identity key as `(x, y)` from `keyOf`, which the trustee registry checked and pinned for good at its own deployment (Trustee registry, Deployment). It computes each quorum as `max(k, n − 1)` ([[0006-trustees]] rule 7) and never reads the trustee registry's `q`, which the deployer chose for ceremony registrations. So a key passes only to a panel that trustee registry pins, and a new panel outside it needs a new trustee registry, a new group registry and so a new Tier 2 scope (Known-weak).

**The current key.** The registry holds the scope's current key, its `key_id` and its panel's `panel_id`, both zero until the initial key is recorded, and the set of every `key_id` it has recorded. `nullifierKey()` returns the current key's two values.

**The initial key.** `recordNullifierKey(bytes record)` takes one record:

```
record   = u64(deadline) ‖ key_id ‖ u8(count) ‖ approval × count
approval = u8(index) ‖ p256-sig
```

`key_id` is `bytes[32]` and `p256-sig` [[trustee-identity]]'s 64-byte `r ‖ s`, so the record is exactly `41 + 65 · count` bytes. Anyone may submit it. The registry checks, in this order, and reverts on the first failure (checks 7 and 8 approval by approval, in record order):

1. **Canonical calldata**, as for the election registry's `registerDefinition` (T-17).
2. **Length**: at least 41 bytes, and exactly `41 + 65 · count`.
3. **No key yet**: the current key is zero. Once any key is recorded, initial or reshared, this call reverts for good (`KeyAlreadyRecorded`).
4. **The deadline**: `block.number ≤ deadline ≤ block.number + MAX_KEY_WINDOW` ([[nullifier-key]], Key context). The first bound is the counting rule's "recorded at or before the run's deadline"; the second can't fail for an honest run, whose seats took part only with the deadline at most `MAX_KEY_WINDOW` ahead.
5. **The key**: `key_id` isn't zero.
6. **Quorum**: `count ≥ max(k, n − 1)`, with `k` and `n` the initial panel's.
7. **The seats**: strictly increasing, the first at least 1 and the last at most `n`, so each seat counts once, never each signature ([[trustee-identity]], Ceremony registration; T-31).
8. **The signatures**: each verifies, under its seat's identity key in the initial panel, on [[nullifier-key]]'s approval statement (Approval) with `l2_chain_id = block.chainid`, `group_registry` this contract, the initial panel's `panel_id`, the approval's `index`, and the record's `key_id` and `deadline`. The registry builds the 101-byte statement and its digest `H(DS("abolish/v1/sig/nullifier-key-approval-3", m))` itself, and verifies as the trustee registry does ([[trustee-identity]], On chain): `1 ≤ r < n_P256` and `1 ≤ s ≤ (n_P256 − 1) / 2`, with `n_P256` P-256's group order, checked itself, then OpenZeppelin's `P256.verify`. Every approval must verify, including any beyond the quorum.

It then makes `key_id` the current key with the initial panel's `panel_id`, adds it to the recorded set and emits `NullifierKeyRecorded`.

**A reshare.** `recordNullifierReshare(bytes record)` takes one record:

```
record = predecessor ‖ new_panel_id ‖ u64(deadline) ‖ key_id ‖ u8(consents) ‖ approval × consents ‖ u8(count) ‖ approval × count
```

with `predecessor`, `new_panel_id` and `key_id` `bytes[32]` and each `approval` as above, so the record is exactly `106 + 65 · (consents + count)` bytes, with `count` the byte at offset `105 + 65 · consents`. Anyone may submit it. The registry checks, in this order (checks 8 and 9 approval by approval, in record order):

1. **Canonical calldata** (T-17).
2. **Length**: at least 105 bytes, at least `106 + 65 · consents`, and exactly `106 + 65 · (consents + count)`.
3. **The predecessor** is the current key, which isn't zero (`NotCurrentKey`). A successor of any other key, an earlier one included, reverts.
4. **The new panel** is pinned by the trustee registry: `panelOf(new_panel_id)` gives a threshold that isn't zero (`UnknownPanel`). It may be the current key's panel, for a refresh ([[nullifier-reshare]], Parties and keys).
5. **The deadline**: `block.number ≤ deadline ≤ block.number + MAX_RESHARE_WINDOW` ([[nullifier-reshare]], Reshare context).
6. **The key**: `key_id` isn't zero and isn't in the recorded set (`KeyAlreadyRecorded`), so no record makes an earlier key current again. An honest successor's `key_id` hashes a transcript whose context binds its predecessor, so it never repeats one.
7. **Quorums**: `consents ≥ max(k, n − 1)`, with `k` and `n` the current key's panel's, and `count ≥ max(k', n' − 1)`, with `k'` and `n'` the new panel's.
8. **The consents**: their seats strictly increasing, from 1 to at most `n`; each verifies, under its seat's identity key in the current key's panel, on [[nullifier-reshare]]'s consent statement (Consent) with `l2_chain_id = block.chainid`, this contract, the current key's `panel_id` as `old_panel_id`, the consent's `index`, and the record's `predecessor`, `new_panel_id`, `key_id` and `deadline`: the 165-byte statement under `abolish/v1/sig/nullifier-reshare-consent`.
9. **The approvals**: their seats strictly increasing, from 1 to at most `n'`; each verifies, under its seat's identity key in the new panel, on [[nullifier-reshare]]'s approval statement (Approval) with `l2_chain_id = block.chainid`, this contract, `new_panel_id`, the approval's `index`, `predecessor`, `key_id` and `deadline`: the 133-byte statement under `abolish/v1/sig/nullifier-reshare-approval-2`.

Signatures are checked as for the initial key, every one of them. It then makes `key_id` the current key with `new_panel_id`, adds it to the recorded set and emits `NullifierKeyReshared`. The predecessor is retired from then on ([[nullifier-reshare]], Approval). Neither call checks or records the sender (T-71).

**What the records buy.** A second counted key, and with it a second nullifier for every document, would need the registry to record two initial keys, or two successors of one key; it records neither, whatever the seats' views of the chain and however many quorums' approvals exist (on an honest L2 history: Known-weak, A rewritten L2 history), so rules 4 and 6 of [[nullifier-key]]'s Approval become defence in depth rather than the guarantee (T-03). Two runs that each reach a quorum before their deadlines both have approvals, and the one recorded first counts: the other's record reverts for good, and its seats erase what it gave them ([[nullifier-key]], Approval). The deadline comes from the statements every approving seat signed, never from the submitter, because the registry can't read it from `key_id`; a quorum's approvals held back past their deadline can't be recorded at all, so the seats that saw no key recorded by the deadline, erased the run's shares and approved another run were right (T-54). The consents put the old panel's agreement on chain: a successor needs `max(k, n − 1)` old seats as well as `q'` new ones, so a panel of an insider's own keys, pinned by the trustee registry, can't take the chain without the old trustees ([[nullifier-reshare]], Security notes, The release alone can't choose the recipients; T-37, T-40).

**Who submits.** Anyone holding a quorum's approvals: the coordinator, or any seat, since every approval and consent is sent to every seat of the run. An honest seat submits the record itself, through the L1 portal, if it isn't recorded `FORCE_LEAD` blocks before the deadline ([[nullifier-key]], Approval, Recording), and every key's transcript is archived before its record is submitted (Known-weak). Withholding a record past the deadline costs the run (liveness only, T-54). Each call checks a quorum's signatures (two quorums' for a reshare), at about the cost of a ceremony registration's approvals without its key set (Trustee registry, Known-weak, Gas), once per key: in the tests, the vectors' initial record with four approvals costs about 186,000 gas with the `P256VERIFY` precompile (1,144,000 on OpenZeppelin's fallback) and their reshare with four consents and three approvals about 207,000 (1,875,000), beyond the transaction's base cost and calldata.

**No member is linked.** The records hold panels, seat indices, signatures, a deadline and key identifiers: nothing of any document, member or poll (T-12).

### Signed messages

Registrars sign [EIP-712](https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-712) typed data, an external format ([[domain-separation]]), so a registrar's signer, and anyone auditing it, sees what is signed in the standard's readable form ([[0004-l2-choice]]). The domain is

```
EIP712Domain(string name,string version,uint256 chainId,address verifyingContract)
name = "Abolish group registry", version = "1", chainId = block.chainid, verifyingContract = the registry
```

and the two message types are

```
AddMembers(uint64 groupId,uint8 tier,uint64 firstIndex,uint256[] commitments)
RemoveMembers(uint64 groupId,uint8 tier,uint64 size,uint64[] indices)
```

with `tier` the group's tier as the registry recorded it, `commitments` and `indices` the record's in record order. As EIP-712 defines them, `hashStruct(s) = keccak256(typeHash ‖ enc(s))`, where each integer is one 32-byte big-endian word and an array is `keccak256` of its elements' words concatenated, and the digest is

```
keccak256(0x19 ‖ 0x01 ‖ hashStruct(domain) ‖ hashStruct(message))
```

The domain binds the chain ID and the registry's address, and the message the group, its tier and, for additions, the position (for removals, `size`), so a signed batch can't be replayed on another chain or deployment, into another group or tier, or at another place in its group ([[verifier]] 2.4; T-30, T-32, T-06). The record carries the signatures, never the digest: the registry and every verifier recompute it.

### Events

Each event goes through the event accumulator, with topic 0 the keccak-256 of its signature:

| Event                                                                                                                | Topics                                         | Data                                                     |
| -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------- |
| `RegistrarsPinned(uint8 threshold, address[] registrars)`                                                            | none beyond topic 0                            | `t` and the addresses, ABI-encoded                       |
| `GroupCreated(uint64 indexed groupId, uint8 indexed tier)`                                                           | `group_id` and the tier, as 32-byte words      | empty                                                    |
| `MembersAdded(uint64 indexed groupId, uint64 indexed firstIndex, bytes record)`                                      | `group_id` and `first_index`, as 32-byte words | the whole addition record, ABI-encoded as `bytes`        |
| `MembersRemoved(uint64 indexed groupId, bytes record)`                                                               | `group_id`, as a 32-byte word                  | the whole removal record, ABI-encoded as `bytes`         |
| `Tier1MemberAdded(bytes record)`                                                                                     | none beyond topic 0                            | the whole Tier 1 addition record, ABI-encoded as `bytes` |
| `SelfPaidGroupCreated(uint64 indexed groupId)`                                                                       | `group_id`, as a 32-byte word                  | empty                                                    |
| `SelfPaidMemberAdded(uint64 indexed groupId, uint64 indexed index, bytes record)`                                    | `group_id` and `index`, as 32-byte words       | the whole self-paid record, ABI-encoded as `bytes`       |
| `NullifierKeyRecorded(bytes32 indexed keyId, bytes record)`                                                          | `key_id`                                       | the whole initial-key record, ABI-encoded as `bytes`     |
| `NullifierKeyReshared(bytes32 indexed predecessor, bytes32 indexed keyId, bytes32 indexed newPanelId, bytes record)` | `predecessor`, `key_id` and `new_panel_id`     | the whole reshare record, ABI-encoded as `bytes`         |

`Tier1MemberAdded` indexes no group: a verifier takes the group from the record ([[vouching]], Counting across the deployment, Checks, `group`), so a topic would be a second copy of it that could disagree. Indexers decode it from the data. `SelfPaidMemberAdded` carries `index` only as a topic, since its record names no position: the registry assigns it, and a verifier checks it against its own count (Verification).

The two nullifier-key events join the contract-event record's tables in a new record version, with the Tier 2 additions, before any registry but the staging deployment's is deployed (Contract-event record; Testnet deployments); no profile pins a version that has them yet. Until then a registry logs them only when a quorum of its initial panel records a key, which no deployment does before that version, so readers of the sequence under earlier versions never meet them; `packages/contracts`' `contract-events.test.ts` compares the registry's other events with versions 7 and 8 and requires these two to be in no version's table yet. Their records' topics repeat fields of the record, and a verifier compares each with its record before using either (Verification).

The registrars are logged at deployment, so they are the registry's first event (which, until 1.2 binds creation code, only the release constructor guarantees: Board anchor, Known-weak, Constructor effects aren't bound).

### Verification

Like the other registries, the group registry is shared by every election, so a verifier replays its whole event sequence from the archive, authenticated by the accumulator ([[verifier]] 4.4; Election registry, The shared event sequence). For each tier group a definition names, the verifier ([[verifier]] 2.4):

- requires the registry's `RegistrarsPinned` to name exactly the set and `t` its release pins, and otherwise fails every group of that registry: a registry deployed from the release's bytecode with other registrars would accept additions the verifier doesn't count, and its removals, which name indices in the registry's numbering, would then zero other members' leaves in the verifier's;
- takes the group's tier and kind from the event that created it: the tier `GroupCreated` logs, for a signed group, or tier 0, for a group `SelfPaidGroupCreated` created (a self-paid group is replayed as below, Self-paid groups). Each `group_id` must be created exactly once across both events, in counter order (the registry's `n`-th creation, of either kind, is group `n`), earlier in the registry's event sequence than any record naming it; a creation that breaks this, or a record naming a group not yet created, fails every definition naming that `group_id` (`group-history`), since the registry can't log one and the group's kind would otherwise be ambiguous (T-71);
- selects the group's records by their events' `group_id` topic, and fails the group (`group-history`) if a record's own `group_id`, or an addition's `first_index`, differs from its event's topic, checked for each record before the replay's checks below: a `group_id` as the replay's `group`, and a `first_index` as `position`;
- **replays the group's records itself**, in log order up to the tier's `root_l2_block` (a rolling tier's through the blocks whose timestamp is at or before `closes_at`, [[membership]], Rolling tier tree), keeping its own member count `c` (from 0) and removed set, and applies to each record every check the registry applies, with no state taken from the chain: the record decodes strictly as above (no forbidden commitment); its digest, recomputed with the chain ID and registry address its release pins and the tier from `GroupCreated`, carries at least the threshold its release pins of approvals, by distinct registrars of the set its release pins, in increasing seat order, every one of which verifies (rules 7 and 8); an addition's `first_index` equals `c` and `c + count ≤ 2³²`, after which its member `i` is leaf `c + i` and `c` grows by `count`; a removal's `size` is at most `c`, its indices are below `size` and none is removed already, after which those leaves are 0. For each record the verifier reports the first failing check in this order, so conforming verifiers name the same reason: decoding (`length`, `batch`, `forbidden-commitment`, `index-order`, `index-range`), the group (`group`), the approvals (`approvals`, `seat`, `signature`), the position or size (`position`, `size`), then removed leaves (`removed`). The registry's own revert order differs; only the verifier's is fixed;
- rebuilds the root and the member count from those leaves as [[membership]] defines them.

A **Tier 1 group** is the exception: anyone may submit its additions, so it is never replayed strictly. Its leaves are its counted additions, decided across the whole registry by [[vouching]] (Counting across the deployment) over every `Tier1MemberAdded` in log order, each judged with the chain ID and registry address the release pins and with `b_A` the block its log carries. The verifier repeats every check the registry makes (Tier 1 additions) as part of those checks and trusts none of them; an addition that fails any check doesn't count and fails nothing. A `MembersAdded` or `MembersRemoved` naming a Tier 1 group, which the registry refuses, adds or removes nothing in it, and so does a `SelfPaidMemberAdded` naming one.

**Self-paid groups** are replayed strictly, like signed ones, with no approvals to check. In log order up to the tier's `root_l2_block` (a rolling tier's as above), keeping its own member count `c` (from 0), the verifier requires of each `SelfPaidMemberAdded` its `group_id` topic selects: the record decodes strictly as above, exactly 40 bytes (`length`) with no forbidden commitment (`forbidden-commitment`); then its own `group_id` equals the topic and names a group `SelfPaidGroupCreated` created (`group`); then its `index` topic equals `c`, with `c < 2³²` (`position`). Leaf `c` is then the record's commitment, and `c` grows by 1. For every kind of record the order of report is the same: decoding first, then `group` (the topic against the record and the group's kind), then the record's own later checks, so a record that names the wrong kind of group and also fails to decode reports its decoding code. A `MembersAdded` or `MembersRemoved` naming a self-paid group, and a `SelfPaidMemberAdded` naming a signed group, are records the registry refuses, so each fails the replay of the group it names, at its decoding code or else at `group`.

**A record that fails any of these checks fails every definition naming the group** (`group-history`, naming the record): the chain then shows a history this registry's rules forbid, so it was rewritten by L2 governance or a false output root (T-71), or comes from code other than the release's (1.2), and the verifier fails closed rather than guess which leaves were meant. On the release's bytecode with the pinned registrars, on an honest chain, no logged record fails them, so no registrant or relay can trigger this (the registry refuses forbidden commitments, so [[membership]]'s leaf-0 rule never applies to this registry's records). Rechecking the signed `first_index` and `size` against the verifier's own count is what makes a rewritten history detectable: a replayed or reordered batch, or one omitted before a later batch, breaks the signed sequence (T-71, T-06, T-13). Two omissions still check, and are caught only by an honest accumulator storage proof (4.4, the T-71 residual): dropping the **latest additions** before `root_l2_block`, which leaves a shorter sequence; and dropping **any removal**, anywhere, since removals don't move the count and carry no sequence number of their own, which keeps a member the registrars removed in the electorate. A per-group counter signed by every record would narrow the second to the tail as well; it isn't worth a second sequence while the first residual stands. A self-paid group's records carry no signature at all, so its replay catches only a history the registry couldn't have logged (a skipped or repeated index, a forbidden commitment, a record in the wrong kind of group): a rewritten chain can add, drop or reorder its additions and renumber them consecutively, and only the accumulator's storage proofs catch that (4.4, the T-71 residual). Reordering self-paid additions across groups changes which copy of a repeated commitment ranks first ([[membership]], Cell tree), which is the same residual.

**Nullifier keys.** A verifier follows the scope's chain of keys by replaying `NullifierKeyRecorded` and `NullifierKeyReshared` in log order, with the trustee registry and the initial panel its release pins (the registry's two immutables, which 1.2 compares with them). It repeats every check the registry makes, with no state taken from the chain: the record decodes strictly as above; its event's topics equal the record's fields; the first record is an initial key and every later one a reshare of the key the one before made current; each deadline is at least the L2 block its log carries and at most `MAX_KEY_WINDOW` or `MAX_RESHARE_WINDOW` beyond it; no `key_id` repeats; and the quorums of distinct seats' signatures verify under the release's pinned panels. It then adds what the registry can't check: each key's transcript, under the key's `key_id`, passes [[nullifier-key]]'s check under version 2 with `max(k, n − 1)` qualified seats, or [[nullifier-reshare]]'s check with its predecessor's result, `max(k, n − 1)` qualified dealers and `max(k', n' − 1)` qualified new seats, and carries the record's `deadline`; and each new panel is one the release pins as a possible successor for the scope, a refresh of the current panel included. A later release keeps every panel an earlier one allowed, or its verifier would stop at a reshare honest seats made. The chain stops at the first record that fails any of this, and that key and every later one don't count. Where the transcripts are published, what a verifier reports and how 2.4 counts Tier 2 additions under the chain are [[P3-4]]'s later steps.

`replayNullifierKeyRecords` in `packages/crypto` performs the registry's part of this replay, reporting the first failing record and the chain before it, which still stands. Each record is judged in this order, so conforming verifiers name the same reason: its length (`length`), its event's topics (`topic`), then the registry's checks in the registry's order: for an initial key, a key already recorded (`key-recorded`), the deadline (`deadline`), a zero `key_id` (`key-id`), the quorum (`quorum`), then approval by approval the seat (`seat`) and the signature (`signature`); for a reshare, a predecessor that isn't the current key (`predecessor`), a panel the trustee registry doesn't pin (`panel`), the deadline, a zero `key_id` (`key-id`) or one already recorded (`key-recorded`), both quorums (`quorum`), then the consents and then the approvals, each by seat and signature. The transcript checks and the release's successor pins are added to it with the verifier's Tier 2 counting ([[P3-4]]'s later steps).

`applyGroupRecords` in `packages/crypto` performs exactly this replay for one group, with vectors for honest sequences and for replayed, reordered, omitted, stale and unsigned records, so independent verifiers agree (T-36).

`packages/crypto` decodes both records, computes both digests, counts a record's valid seats and replays a group (`group-registry.ts`), for the verifier and for the signing service; the contract and that code are checked against the same vectors. It also decodes the self-paid record (`decodeSelfPaidAddition`) and replays a self-paid group (`applySelfPaidRecords`), and `firstOccurrence` ranks [[membership]]'s first-occurrence rule, signed groups first and then self-paid additions in chain order; `groupRegistryEvents` reads both self-paid events, and the ballot client's and the creator's tier replays (`tierMembership`, `statedTierElectorate`) take a group's kind from the event that created it and replay each kind as above ([[P3-2]] part 5c). The self-paid call, that code and `packages/contracts`' tests are checked against `vectors/self-paid.json` (Vectors) and version 7's records of the events it emits (Contract-event record, Vectors).

The Tier 1 call is checked against `vectors/vouch.json` and `vectors/vouch-count.json` ([[vouching]], Vectors), from Semaphore's own prover and an implementation that shares no code with ours: every scope and message as the registry computes them; rule 7 alone over every addition `vouch.json` accepts or refuses at `proof`, naming the same vouch and proof (its root blocks are off the grid, so the whole call refuses them at rule 5); its rejected encodings, each at the registry's first rule that meets it; and `vouch-count.json`'s deployment replayed block by block in log order, in which the registry accepts exactly the additions the counting rule counts or refuses only at `root-mismatch`, `slot-spent` or `member`, and refuses every other one at its rule for the spec's failing check.

**No member is linked to a poll.** The registry takes no election identifier, definition or nullifier, and an addition names only a group, a position and commitments. An election names a group and a root, never a member; a ballot proves membership in zero knowledge and carries a nullifier unrelated to the commitment ([[membership]], Scope). So nothing on chain joins a member to the polls they vote in (T-12, T-13). When a commitment is added is public, to about the registration period of the batch that added it ([[registration]], The batch); T-13 records that residual.

### Vectors

`vectors/nullifier-key-registry.json` (format `abolish-nullifier-key-registry-vectors/1`), printed by `packages/crypto/scripts/nullifier-key-registry-vectors.py`, an implementation written from this page, [[nullifier-key]] and [[nullifier-reshare]] with Python's standard library only, on `trustee-vectors.py`'s P-256 ECDSA (checked against RFC 6979 first), gives: three pinned panels' keys, as compressed keys and as the `(x, y)` the trustee registry returns, with each `panel_id` and quorum, one of them pinned by no trustee registry; the three statements (Nullifier keys), each with its digest `H(DS(tag, m))` and every seat's signature; an initial record, a reshare and a refresh, decoded field by field; and replays of the registry's log, each with the chain of keys it records or the first record it refuses and the code of the check that refuses it. The cases cover an honest chain of an initial key, a reshare to another panel and a refresh; deadlines at both edges of the window; every length rule; topics that differ from the record; a second initial key, before and after a reshare; a deadline passed or too far ahead; a zero `key_id`; a successor making an earlier key current again; each quorum one short; a repeated, out-of-order, zero or out-of-range seat; a signature by another seat, over another deadline, panel or key, under a retired tag, with a high `s`, or one bad beyond the quorum; consents by the new panel or to another panel; a reshare with no key recorded, of a retired key, and to a panel the trustee registry doesn't pin; and a chain stopping at its first failing record. Key identifiers there are labels' hashes, since the registry checks no transcript. `packages/crypto` (`nullifier-key-registry.ts`) consumes all of it, and so does `packages/contracts` (`NullifierKeys.t.sol`): a trustee registry and the group registry deployed at the vectors' addresses on their chain, each statement and digest built by the registry's own code, and every replay submitted at the blocks its log carries, in which the registry records exactly the replay's chain and refuses the failing record at the error for the replay's code. A `topic` failure is a log the registry can't emit, since it takes its topics from the record: the registry accepts that record and logs topics equal to its fields.

`vectors/group-registry.json` (format `abolish-group-registry-vectors/1`, see [[vectors/README|vectors]]), printed by `packages/crypto/scripts/group-registry-vectors.py`, an implementation written from this page and the EIP-712 and secp256k1 standards with Python's standard library only, gives: the domain separator and type hashes; addition and removal records with their digests, signed by fixed test keys, and each approval's recovered signer; records each check refuses; and the forbidden commitments. TypeScript (`packages/crypto`) and Solidity (`packages/contracts`) tests both consume it.

`vectors/self-paid.json` (format `abolish-self-paid-vectors/1`), printed by `packages/crypto/scripts/self-paid-vectors.py`, an implementation written from this page and [[membership]] (Cell tree) with Python's standard library only, reusing `tier-tree-vectors.py`'s self-checked Poseidon2 and tier tree, gives: valid self-paid records and records each stateless check refuses (`length`, `forbidden-commitment`); one self-paid group's replays, honest and refused at each of `length`, `forbidden-commitment`, `group` and `position`, including the order of report when a record fails two; and tiers of signed and self-paid groups, each with the registry's log in chain order, every group's leaves before and after the first-occurrence ranking, region counts and each cell's root, member count and paths. Its cases cover a self-paid copy of a signed member in a lower `group_id`, a signed copy landing after a self-paid registration, two self-paid copies ranked by chain order against their `group_id`, a removed signed occurrence, a repeat within a self-paid group, interleaved groups over three regions, a self-paid region that is a cell of its own, and a self-paid copy keeping its region below the floor. TypeScript (`packages/crypto`) consumes all of it; Solidity (`packages/contracts`) consumes the records.

`Poseidon2(0, 1)` can't be computed by the generator, which has no Poseidon: the value was computed with `poseidon-lite` 0.3.0 (circomlib's constants), which in the same run gave circomlib's published `Poseidon2(1, 2) = 0x115cc0f5…189a` and the tier tree's `Z_1 = Poseidon2(0, 0)`. P1-17b's Poseidon, built from circomlib's parameters, MUST check this value.

### Known-weak

- **Tier 0 is the registrars' word** (T-06, not mitigated): any `t` registrars can add any commitments. What this registry adds is that each addition is attributable to `t` named keys, that no one else (no relay, no single registrar) can add, replay or reorder one, that L2 governance rewriting the history to do so is detected by every verifier (Verification; dropping the latest additions, or any removal, isn't), and that group sizes are public.
- **The quorum is held online.** Tier 0 registration is automatic (one Turnstile solve per registration and no account, [[registration]]), so the signing service holds `t` registrar keys hot, and seizing it is seizing Tier 0 additions (T-69). Hardware-held keys would need a person per registration.
- **The registrars can't rotate.** A lost or compromised key means a new registry, and every member registers again in its new groups. That is the cost of a set a verifier can check without the Safe's history.
- **Duplicates are the signing path's to refuse** (Additions): a repeated commitment overstates `group_size` by one. Across signed and self-paid groups the registry refuses none, and the first-occurrence rule's ranking resolves them ([[membership]], Cell tree).
- **A self-paid registrant can be moved** (T-16, Self-paid additions): by whoever sees its transaction pending (the RPC endpoint and the sequencer, or anyone at all for a call through the L1 portal or after a reverted attempt) and lands a self-paid copy in another region first, and by the registrars, or a compromised signing service, signing a copy at any time, since every signed occurrence ranks first (T-06, T-69). The client detects either and can register again only under a new identity secret ([[registration]], Outcome). Ranking both kinds by chain order instead would let a cheap self-paid copy that front-runs a signed batch move signed registrants, who are most voters ([[0024-tier0-registration]], option D).
- **Paid floods of self-paid additions** (T-52, T-53, T-17; Self-paid additions, Junk): anyone can add any commitment that isn't forbidden to any self-paid group, for `SELF_PAID_FEE` and the gas of one call, with no Turnstile solve. Each is a counted leaf of every definition that lists the group, an event the operator must board on each such election's board (so the cost lands again per election, on us), a record every ballot client of those elections reads before casting, and about 31 chosen bytes in the archive. The fee makes a million additions cost 200 ETH beyond gas, which a state actor can still pay. Against a flood that is paid, the operator publishes a new self-paid group for the region in a new release and lists it in later definitions instead of the flooded one: polls already defined keep the flood, and the flooded group's honest registrants are no longer in new polls until they add themselves again, with a new fee (T-25). A rolling poll ([[P2-17]]) that lists the group keeps taking the flood until its close, since its board carries the group's additions through close, each before the last boundary appending a leaf to its steps and each through close counted in growth (Election registry, The shared event sequence, Rolling tiers): the new group helps only polls defined after it, and an open rolling poll's boarding, verification and clients carry whatever is paid for before it closes. Each of those additions is a required board entry ([[verifier]] 4.4), and 4.4 fails only on one still missing at the seal (`lastSegment`, [[election-definition]], The seal), days after close; they enter no counted set, so `BOARDING_WINDOW`, which attributes `tally-late`, never applies to them. So a flood the operator can't board in full by the seal fails the poll (T-53), unattributed by the verifier, as a missing required event always is; a fixed poll's creator sees a flood before `root_l2_block` and can leave the group out, a rolling poll's can't. The rate is bounded only by the fee, the call's gas and the L2's block gas limit, and the operator's boarding throughput against it isn't measured yet. Before any registry is deployed beyond a testnet, [[P4-3]] shows the operator can board the most the L2's gas limit allows over a poll's life by its seal, or a rolling tier must list only groups whose additions are rate-limited (signed batches or Tier 1), or what a rolling poll must board is capped. At Tier 0 a Sybil gains no identities from this that registering many doesn't already give it (T-01). Whether 0.0002 ETH is the right price is for review before any registry is deployed beyond a testnet.
- **Self-paid groups have no removals** (Self-paid additions, No removals): a lost or stolen self-paid secret stays a member for the registry's life. And a removal from a signed group doesn't keep its commitment out of Tier 0, since anyone can self-add it again (T-18).
- **A recorded key whose transcript fails stops the chain** (T-54, T-03). The registry records whatever a quorum signs, so `max(k, n − 1)` corrupt seats of the initial panel can record an initial key whose transcript fails the check, and a verifier then counts no Tier 2 key at all; for a reshare it takes `max(k, n − 1)` corrupt old seats and `max(k', n' − 1)` corrupt new seats. Either quorum can already deny the scope (a key needs `k` seats to answer), so this adds a way to fail, not a weaker uniqueness: no record makes a second key count. Honest quorums stop the chain the same way if a key's transcript is lost before it is published, which is why it is archived before its record is submitted (where, a later [[P3-4]] step), or if they reshare to a panel the verifier's release doesn't allow, which honest old seats' consent rule 6 forbids. Recovering means a new group registry and so a new Tier 2 scope.
- **Panels are the trustee registry's, fixed at its deployment.** A key passes only to a panel the trustee registry pins, and that registry adds no panel after deployment, so the scope's possible successors are fixed when it is deployed: a new panel outside them means new trustee and group registries, a new scope, and every Tier 2 member registering again. And the registry accepts any successor panel the trustee registry pins, which the old quorum's consent gates on chain; only the verifier holds the release's pin of which panel succeeds which.
