A front-runner can't attach another root to an honest forced ballot: the root is bound in the relay's call hash, a deposit carrying another root matches no call (`relay-unmatched`), and only L2 governance can make one; a second relay call with the record and another root is refused here, since a proof fixes its root (Rolling ballots, One root per record), and leaves a word with no event.

A deposit that reverts here leaves the relay's call recorded with no event, reported and never failed (L1 relay, Verification, Calls with no event), as on the other relayed call. **Gas** is a rolling ballot's on `submitRollingBallot` plus the four words of call fields, about a version-4 relayed ballot's of the same shape.

### Close statements

Each seat of the default panel's registered key set sends its close statement through the board path and through the contract path ([[close]], What a node does), the latter by `submitCloseStatement(bytes record)`, whose calldata is canonical (Calls). The call bounds what it archives as the operator bounds the board path ([[close]], Boarding; T-17), and reverts on the first of these, in order. A record byte-identical to one the board already accepted is first the no-op of Calls, Repeats, checking nothing: a node's retry resends its stored bytes, and so does anyone copying a statement from the board path.

1. **Decoding**: the record decodes strictly as a close statement of version 1 ([[close]], Close statement, Decoding), the version contract-event version 3 fixes: a record shorter than its type is `truncated` at 0, and one ending inside a field `truncated` at that field; a type other than `0x0015` is `unexpected-record-type` if [[versioning]] lists it and `unknown-record-type` otherwise, both at 0; a version other than 1 is `unknown-version` at 2; a signature with `r` or `s` out of range ([[trustee-identity]], Signatures) is `non-canonical` at 116; bytes after the signature are `trailing-bytes` at 180.
2. **`statement-stray`**: the election registry recorded no parameters for its `definition_hash` (`ballot_version` 0).
3. **`statement-seat`**: `signer` is a seat of the registered key set `keySetOf(stub_hash, panel_id)` returns for `ceremony(definition_hash)`, the default panel's (Election registry, Parameters). Seat 0 fails, and so does any seat outside the set.
4. **`statement-signature`**: the signature verifies under the seat's per-election signing key, `signingKeyOf(stub_hash, panel_id, signer)`, over the record's first 116 bytes under `abolish/v1/sig/close-statement` ([[close]], Close statement), with the digest computed on chain as [[trustee-identity]] (On chain) requires.
5. **`statement-limit`**: the board already accepted two statements of this `signer` for this `definition_hash`, or one with the same claim (`l2_number`, `l2_hash`). So at most two per seat and election are archived, the second only with another claim, which shows equivocation, exactly what the operator boards ([[close]], Boarding).

Then it records the statement's claim against its seat and definition hash and emits `CloseStatementSubmitted` (Events). The board judges neither `L_δ`, nor the claim, nor the time: its own L2 view is no evidence of `head(L_δ)`, and the statement is in time by its submission time ([[close]], Window), which the verifier reads from the event's block. A statement the board refuses can still count through the board path, and one it accepts counts only if [[close]] (Close head) finds it valid. Anyone can call: the signature binds the seat, so a statement the call accepts says only what its seat signed.

What a seat can still archive through the call is two statements' chosen bytes per election, 40 bytes of claim and a grindable signature `r` each, as through the board path (T-17).

### Availability records

Each seat of an election's panels sends its availability attestations and complaints ([[availability]], Records) through the contract path only, never the board path, since the board copy is what may be withheld ([[availability]], Contract path; [[0029-board-data-availability]], option D): by `submitAvailabilityRecord(bytes record)`, which takes either, whose calldata is canonical (Calls). `AvailabilityCheckLib` (`packages/contracts`) runs these steps in order, and the call reverts on the first failure. A record byte-identical to one the board already accepted is first the no-op of Calls, Repeats, checking nothing, so a seat that resends its record until it is recorded ([[availability]], Windows) never reads a failure.

1. **Decoding**: the record decodes strictly as an attestation (`0x001b`) or a complaint (`0x001c`) of version 1 ([[availability]], Decoding), the version contract-event version 8 fixes, each field read and validated before the next: a record ending inside a field is `truncated` at that field (at 0 for one shorter than its type, at 2 for one without its version); any other type is `unexpected-record-type` if it is one of `0x0001` to `0x001a` and `unknown-record-type` otherwise, both at 0; a version other than 1 is `profile-mismatch` at 2, as [[availability]]'s decoder reports it; a `panel` or `point` other than 1 or 2 is `invalid-enum` at 35 or 37. In an attestation, a roots count above 32 is `length-over-max` at its count (offset 70), before any root is read, a count other than 0 at point 1 `non-canonical` there, and a root that isn't above the one before `non-canonical` at that root; in a complaint, a `cid` (offset 70) whose first four bytes are neither `0x01 0x55 0x12 0x20` nor `0x01 0x70 0x12 0x20` is `non-canonical` there. A signature with `r` or `s` out of range ([[trustee-identity]], Signatures) is `non-canonical` at its offset (`74 + 32·r` in an attestation of `r` roots, 106 in a complaint), and bytes after it `trailing-bytes` at the record's length.
2. **`availability-stray`**: the election registry recorded no parameters for its `definition_hash` (`ballot_version` 0).
3. **`availability-seat`**: `seat` is a seat of the panel key set of the record's `panel`: for panel 1, the registered key set `keySetOf(stub_hash, panel_id)` returns for `ceremony(definition_hash)`, the default panel's (Election registry, Parameters); for panel 2, the author key set `authorKeySetOf(definition_hash)` returns (Election registry, Author panel registration), empty for a poll with no author panel. Seat 0 fails, and so does any seat outside the set. Panel 2's branch is specified in [[P2-16]] part 5a and built in part 5c-4; the registry refused every author panel until part 5e-5d ([[availability]], Contract path).
4. **`availability-signature`**: the signature verifies under the seat's per-election signing key in its panel key set, `signingKeyOf(stub_hash, panel_id, seat)` for panel 1 and `authorSigningKeyOf(definition_hash, seat)` for panel 2, never its identity key, over the record before its signature, header included (the `74 + 32·r` bytes of an attestation, the 106 of a complaint), under `abolish/v1/sig/availability-attestation` or `abolish/v1/sig/availability-complaint`, with the digest computed on chain as [[trustee-identity]] (On chain) requires.
5. **`availability-root`** (attestations only): each entry of `roots` is the record hash of an obligation root the board accepted for the same `definition_hash` and the attestation's own `panel`, as a slot of that panel keeps it (Delivery records, step 8; Delivery records (version 2), step 8). So the list names only roots the contract path holds, each of the panel that judges it ([[delivery]], Leaf availability (version 2)), and carries no byte the seat chooses beyond which of them it names ([[availability]], Contract path; T-17). A root the board holds is named by any seat of its panel that holds its leaves, its own seat's or another's.
6. **`availability-repeat`**: the board already accepted a record of the call's type for this `definition_hash`, `panel`, `seat` and `point`. The first record of each type that passes the steps above fills its slot, and only a byte-identical repeat of it is accepted after (the no-op above), so a seat archives at most one attestation and one complaint per point, four records per election, and since only a record whose signature verifies reaches this step, no one but the seat can fill its slot ([[availability]], Contract path; T-17, T-52). A refused record fills nothing. A complaint and an attestation of one point fill different slots, so a seat that complained and then fetched the extent still attests ([[availability]], What a seat does).

Then it records the slot and emits `AvailabilityAttestationSubmitted` for an attestation or `AvailabilityComplaintSubmitted` for a complaint (Events), whose seat topic is the record's `seat`, at offset 36, never its `panel` (the topics name no panel: the record does). The board judges neither `checkpoint_hash` nor `cid`, which name L1 data the L2 doesn't read, nor the time: a record is in time by its submission time, the timestamp of the block that emitted its event, which [[availability]]'s check reads with the record (Availability check), so a record forced through L1 by its cutoff counts whatever the sequencer did. Anyone can call: the signature binds the seat, so a record the call accepts says only what its seat signed.

What a seat can still archive through the call is, per election, at most four records: 32 chosen bytes in each `checkpoint_hash`, 32 in a complaint's CID digest and a grindable signature `r` in each, within what a registered seat could disclose anyway, and attributed to it by its signature ([[availability]], Contract path; T-17, T-14).

### Events

Each event goes through the event accumulator, with topic 0 the keccak-256 of its signature, and carries its record whole as `bytes`, so the delivery check and [[verifier]] 5 read the event as the record itself ([[delivery]], Direct submit):

| Event                                                                                                                                                                                | Topics                                                                             | Data                                                                                                                                                       |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `BallotSubmitted(bytes32 indexed definitionHash, bytes32 indexed ballotHash, bytes record)`                                                                                          | The record's `definition_hash` and its `ballot_hash`, recomputed (Ballots, step 9) | The ballot record, ABI-encoded                                                                                                                             |
| `ShareReceiptSubmitted(bytes32 indexed definitionHash, bytes32 indexed ballotHash, bytes record)`                                                                                    | The record's `definition_hash` and `ballot_hash`                                   | The share receipt (164 bytes, 165 at version 2), ABI-encoded                                                                                               |
| `SharePosted(bytes32 indexed definitionHash, bytes32 indexed ballotHash, bytes record)`                                                                                              | The record's `definition_hash` and `ballot_hash`                                   | The share posting, ABI-encoded                                                                                                                             |
| `ShareComplaintSubmitted(bytes32 indexed definitionHash, bytes32 indexed ballotHash, bytes record)`                                                                                  | The record's `definition_hash` and `ballot_hash`                                   | The share complaint (228 bytes, 229 at version 2), ABI-encoded                                                                                             |
| `ShareReceiptBatchSubmitted(bytes32 indexed definitionHash, uint8 indexed seat, bytes record)`                                                                                       | The batch's `definition_hash` and `seat`                                           | The share-receipt batch (`104 + 64·c` bytes, `105 + 64·c` at version 2), ABI-encoded                                                                       |
| `ShareComplaintBatchSubmitted(bytes32 indexed definitionHash, uint8 indexed seat, bytes record)`                                                                                     | The batch's `definition_hash` and `seat`                                           | The share-complaint batch (`104 + 128·c` bytes, `105 + 128·c` at version 2), ABI-encoded                                                                   |
| `RelayedBallotSubmitted(bytes32 indexed definitionHash, bytes32 indexed ballotHash, uint64 index, uint64 l1Block, uint64 stamp, uint64 gasLimit, bytes record)`                      | The record's `definition_hash` and its `ballot_hash`, recomputed                   | The relay call's index, L1 block, stamp and gas limit as the deposit named them, then the ballot record, ABI-encoded                                       |
| `RollingBallotSubmitted(bytes32 indexed definitionHash, bytes32 indexed ballotHash, bytes32 root, bytes record)`                                                                     | The record's `definition_hash` and its `ballot_hash`, recomputed                   | The root the submitter supplied, then the version-5 ballot record, ABI-encoded                                                                             |
| `RelayedRollingBallotSubmitted(bytes32 indexed definitionHash, bytes32 indexed ballotHash, uint64 index, uint64 l1Block, uint64 stamp, uint64 gasLimit, bytes32 root, bytes record)` | The record's `definition_hash` and its `ballot_hash`, recomputed                   | The relay call's index, L1 block, stamp and gas limit as the deposit named them, the root the relay carried, then the version-5 ballot record, ABI-encoded |
| `CloseStatementSubmitted(bytes32 indexed definitionHash, uint8 indexed signer, bytes record)`                                                                                        | The statement's `definition_hash` and `signer`                                     | The close statement (180 bytes), ABI-encoded                                                                                                               |
| `ObligationRootSubmitted(bytes32 indexed definitionHash, uint8 indexed seat, bytes record)`                                                                                          | The root's `definition_hash` and `seat`                                            | The obligation root (173 bytes, 174 at version 2), ABI-encoded                                                                                             |
| `AvailabilityAttestationSubmitted(bytes32 indexed definitionHash, uint8 indexed seat, bytes record)`                                                                                 | The attestation's `definition_hash` and `seat`                                     | The availability attestation (`138 + 32·r` bytes), ABI-encoded                                                                                             |
| `AvailabilityComplaintSubmitted(bytes32 indexed definitionHash, uint8 indexed seat, bytes record)`                                                                                   | The complaint's `definition_hash` and `seat`                                       | The availability complaint (170 bytes), ABI-encoded                                                                                                        |

The topics repeat what the record says, so a reader can find an election's records by topic; the record decides, and a record whose topic disagrees with its bytes fails the contract-event record's check (`event-data`), so 4.4 fails against the deployment (Contract-event record, What the board frames). The first four events are archived under contract-event versions 2 to 12, the two of the close rule under versions 3 to 12, the two batch events under versions 4 to 12, the root event under versions 5 to 12, the two availability events under versions 8 to 12, the rolling ballot event under versions 10 to 12, and the relayed rolling ballot event under versions 11 and 12; those two are the only ones that carry a version-5 ballot (Contract-event record, Event tables (version 10), (version 11) and (version 12), whose board rows are version 11's). No version up to 8 frames a version-4 ballot or a version-2 delivery record, so the board accepts them only together with version 9, which frames both under every one of these events (Contract-event record, Event tables (version 9); [[P2-16]] part 5), and which every profile naming such a board pins. Every record an event carries passed the board's checks; none is a ballot entry or a board-path record, and none counts until the verifier's own checks pass. The board's sequence is shared by every election its registry creates, as the registry's is (Election registry, The shared event sequence): a verifier replays it whole and requires board entries only for the events that name its election.

### Vectors

`packages/contracts` runs every proof and decode vector of `vectors/membership-proof.json` through `MembershipProofLib` (each gets the spec's verdict, and each broken point its offset), its field mappings, and every scope and `F` vector of `vectors/membership.json`, including the three rejected scopes. It also checks `A.y + p`, `C.y + p` and a `C` off the curve at their offsets, and runs under the `osaka` EVM as well as `cancun`. Fuzz tests: no one-bit change to an honest proof, no other value of one signal, and no arbitrary input passes, and none reverts.

The ballot check runs every record of `vectors/ballot.json`, `vectors/ballot-v2.json`, `vectors/ballot-v3-records.json` and `vectors/ballot-v4-records.json` against parameters taken from the file's own election (its definition's panel, questions and ballot pin, its tiers' or cells' roots; for version 5, the questions the file lists beside the definition), with its listed key set: each admitted ballot gives the file's `ballot_hash`, `M` and tier, a ballot the verifier rejects only at `option-proof` or `sum-proof` (for version 3, a range proof naming its question) is admitted, and every other gets the file's code; version 4's are judged against both of the ballot's listed key sets, the default and the author panel's (the author key set judged on its own: removing any author seat the every-seat ballot fills refuses it, whatever the default set), and its author half's shape against each other author threshold, panel size and no author panel; every decode rejection gets its code at its offset, except that `0x000c` is a type the board knows (`unexpected-record-type`, where the file, which knows only `0x0002`, says unknown). Every committed vote of `vectors/ballot-validity.json` and `vectors/ballot-v3.json` frames and has the file's shape verdict, each version-3 vote's ballot hash is the file's, and each codec rejection of a committed vote is refused with its code (and, for version 3, its offset). A test runs it against a real election registry, and the tests run under the `osaka` EVM as well as `cancun`. Fuzz tests: no one-byte change to an honest record (of version 1, and of version 4 with both halves) and no arbitrary record, or arbitrary body after an honest header, is admitted, and every refusal is one of the library's two errors.

`test/BoardBallotV4.t.sol` runs every ballot of `vectors/ballot-v4-records.json` through `submitBallot`, each on a fresh board, against stubs set from the file's elections, with the default key set read through the ceremony and the author key set through `authorKeySetOf`: each one the board admits is emitted exactly as `vectors/contract-event-v9.json` records it (its topics and data, from the independent generator), a repeat emitting nothing, and every other is refused with the file's code; each decode rejection with an offset gets its code there; the every-seat ballot is refused once the registry's author key set loses a seat or is empty; both relayed version-4 ballots of `contract-event-v9.json` are accepted from the alias with their deposits' fields and emitted as the file records them; and `ballotCheck` is the board's first creation, with `BallotCheck`'s runtime code. It runs under `osaka` as well as `cancun`.

`test/BoardDeliveryV2.t.sol` runs every record of `vectors/delivery-v2.json`, of both panels, through its call against stubs set from the file's election (its parameters from Alice's version-4 record, whose committed vote and ballot hash are the file's) and both panels' seats, the default panel's through the trustee registry and the author panel's through `authorKeySetOf` and `authorSigningKeyOf`: each is emitted exactly as `vectors/contract-event-v9.json` records it, a repeat emitting nothing (postings carry Alice's record with the pairing mocked, since that file's ballots carry no membership proof); every decode rejection gets its code at the offset Delivery records (version 2) gives, a receipt whose length isn't its version byte's being `ReceiptBatchLength` first. Both panels' receipts frame in one `submitReceipts` call, and a version-1 receipt after them is framed as 164 bytes and refused as `profile-mismatch`, naming its position. Each panel's record is refused once its own panel's key set lacks the seat, or for the author panel holds none, whatever the other set says; an author receipt is checked under the author signing key, never the default seat's of the same index; one panel's signature relabelled as the other's, and version 1's tag over version 2's statement, are `delivery-signature`; a posting relabelled to the other panel, or to another seat, is `posting-hash`. `vectors/intake-v4.json`'s ballots carry real membership proofs, so their postings run unmocked: every seat's ciphertext of both panels with the case's record is accepted, one panel's ciphertext posted as the other's is `posting-hash`, and a ballot the ballot check refuses carries no posting, with that check's code, before its ciphertext is compared. A one-byte change anywhere in a receipt or complaint of either panel is refused (fuzz), and the version-2 batches and roots of `contract-event-v9.json`, and the same records labelled version 1, are `unknown-version` at 2. It runs under `osaka` as well as `cancun`. It also runs every record of `vectors/delivery-batch-v2.json` (both panels' receipt and complaint batches, receipt roots of seat 1 of each panel, an author complaint root and a full author batch of 1,024 pairs) through its call, each emitted exactly as `contract-event-v9.json` records it, its seat topic the byte at 36, a repeat emitting nothing; every decode rejection of that file with its code at its offset; the root slots per panel (author seat 1's first receipt root filling its slot so its other two are `root-repeat`, while default seat 1's receipt root and author seat 2's complaint root fill their own, and a refused root filling nothing); each panel's key set and signing key for a batch, a complaint batch and a root (an author seat outside the author key set, no author panel, the default seat's key of the same index, the record relabelled to the default panel, and version 1's tag over version 2's statement, the record re-signed under its own tag passing); every batch and root under a pinned ballot version 5 `unknown-version`; attestations naming only their own panel's roots (an author attestation naming a default root, and a default one naming an author root, `availability-root`; the author attestation under its own key accepted and a second refused `availability-repeat`); and fuzzing of one-byte changes to both panels' batches and roots. Under `osaka`, where P-256 goes through `P256VERIFY`, it also binds [[delivery]]'s forced-path figures (Capacity) to version 2's calls: a full receipt batch of `RECEIPTS_PER_CALL` pairs, a full complaint batch of `COMPLAINTS_PER_CALL` entries and a root with dense calldata, each by a default seat and by an author seat, cold and with their intrinsic gas, cost at most `RECEIPT_CALL_GAS`, `COMPLAINT_CALL_GAS` and `ROOT_CALL_GAS` less 30,000 of headroom for real registries (version 1's cost about 2,000 more than the stubs, measured without Solidity's copy of the calldata), on a board that has already accepted a record, and new records of the same seat are accepted with exactly that figure as their gas limit: about 3,847,000 and 3,840,000 for the receipt batches, 4,190,000 and 4,182,000 for the complaint batches, and 162,000 and 155,000 for the roots, default and author. The stubs stand in for the registries there, as they did while the election registry refused every author panel (until [[P2-16]] part 5e-5d).

`vectors/delivery-v5.json` (format `abolish-delivery-contract-vectors/1`, see [[vectors/README|vectors]]) gives a rolling poll's delivery records on the contract path (Delivery records (ballot version 5)), from an independent generator: its elections (two rolling ones of `ballot-v5-records.json`, with and without an author panel, and `intake-v4.json`'s fixed one pinning ballot version 4), each panel's seats with their KEM and signing keys, version-5 ballots with real membership proofs and real ciphertexts to every seat of both panels (one proved against another step's root than the one it names), rolling posting cases (each with its root, posting, ballot, key sets where they differ from the election's, block time where it matters, snarkjs's pairing verdict and the board's result or the code, offset and check that refuses it: the posting's delivery steps or the ballot check), the call's canonical calldata and each way it departs from that, and receipts, complaints, batches and a root of version 2 and version 1 through their calls. Part 5's `test/BoardRolling.t.sol` runs every posting case through its call, each on a fresh board, against stubs set from the file's elections and key sets (the default panel's through the trustee registry, the author panel's through `authorKeySetOf` and `authorSigningKeyOf`): each accepted posting is emitted as `SharePosted` with the posting alone, exactly as `vectors/contract-event-v10.json` frames it, and resent with another root is the no-op; every other is refused with the file's code, from the check the file names, at its offset; each calldata vector is accepted exactly when the file says it is canonical, `NonCanonicalCalldata` otherwise; and every record of `records` is accepted or refused through its call with the file's code at its position. `packages/crypto/tests/delivery-v5.test.ts` reads it now, through `packages/crypto`'s record codecs, its Groth16 verifier, its public posting check and its signature check.

`test/Board.t.sol` runs every ballot of `vectors/ballot.json` and `vectors/ballot-v3-records.json` through `submitBallot` under its file-listed key set, read through the ceremony, the largest version-3 record a definition allows (below), and every delivery record of `vectors/delivery.json`'s elections through the contract path (each posting with the ballot record it binds, built from the file's ballot and checked against its `ballot_hash`), against stubs set from those files' elections and key sets. Each delivery decode rejection of `vectors/delivery.json` gets its code at the offset the layouts above give, except that a receipt one byte short or long, or a complaint sent as a receipt, fails the batch's length first (`ReceiptBatchLength`). Every receipt check of `vectors/share-receipt.json` is accepted exactly when the file says it verifies. Each refusal code and the checking order have tests, as do a byte-identical repeat in and across calls emitting nothing, a failing receipt reverting its whole batch, canonical calldata on every call, each event's exact topics, data and accumulator step, and the board reading a real election registry's ceremony and a real trustee registry's key set and signing keys; the tests run under the `osaka` EVM, where P-256 goes through its precompile, as well as `cancun`. Fuzz tests: no arbitrary input to any call is accepted, every refusal is one of the board's errors, and no one-byte change to an honest receipt or complaint, or to a posting outside `m`, is accepted. It also binds [[delivery]]'s forced-path figures (Capacity) from `vectors/delivery-obligation.json`: a call of `RECEIPTS_PER_CALL` receipts fits a deposit's 120,000 bytes, both call figures fit a deposit's 20,000,000 gas, and, in the run under `osaka`, which must find `P256VERIFY` at 0x100, a call of 256 distinct receipts and a complaint call cost at most `RECEIPT_CALL_GAS` and `COMPLAINT_CALL_GAS` with their intrinsic gas (EIP-2028's price or EIP-7623's floor, whichever is higher), and more than 90% and 80% of them, so the capacity check is neither exceeded nor far looser than the board. Each call is measured on its own with the board's and both registries' accounts and slots cold (`vm.cool`), against the stubs and against real registries, and each succeeds with exactly its figure as its gas limit, as a deposit gives it. An invariant suite (`test/invariant/Board.invariant.t.sol`) interleaves honest, repeated, changed and mixed submissions of every type and holds that the event count is the number of distinct records accepted, that every emitted record passes its check again and is emitted once, that a refused record is never remembered, and that accepting one record never makes another passing record fail (T-25, T-54).

`test/Board.t.sol` also runs every batch of `vectors/delivery-batch.json` through `submitReceiptBatch` against the stubs set from `delivery.json` (the same panel and definition), each emitted with its definition hash and seat as topics and the data `vectors/contract-event-v4.json` records for it, a repeat emitting nothing; every decode rejection with its code at its offset; a stray definition, seats 0 and 4, another seat's key and the single receipt's tag refused in order; canonical calldata; and fuzzing of arbitrary batches and of one-byte changes to an honest one, of either kind. `test/invariant/Board.invariant.t.sol` interleaves both batch calls with every other call (honest batches of `delivery-batch.json`, repeated, one byte changed, and the complaint batch whose `D` frames but isn't a point), and its invariants hold for both batch events: one event per distinct record, each re-checked, nothing remembered from a refusal, and no acceptance making another honest record fail. Its forced-path test measures a full batch of 1,024 dense pairs cold against real registries.

`test/Board.t.sol` also runs every root of `vectors/delivery-root.json` through `submitObligationRoot` on its own, against the same stubs, each emitted with its definition hash and seat as topics and the data `vectors/contract-event-v5.json` records for it, a repeat emitting nothing; every decode rejection with its code at its offset; a stray definition, seats 0 and 4, another seat's key, a batch's tag, a root through the batch call and a batch through the root call, a version-1 root under ballot version 4 (`profile-mismatch`) and under ballot version 5 (`unknown-version`) refused, and the honest root accepted after; a raw and a dag-pb CID either side of the block boundary for both kinds, each accepted with the codec its size gives and refused with the other, and the largest count; the slots (a root with a broken signature filling nothing, seat 1's second receipt root refused as `root-repeat` while the first's repeat is the no-op, seat 2's receipt and complaint roots each in its own slot, seats 1 and 3's other slots still empty); canonical calldata; and fuzzing of arbitrary roots, of one-byte changes to an honest one, and of any signed kind, count and root accepted once with a second root of the seat and kind then refused. `test/invariant/Board.invariant.t.sol` interleaves the root call with every other call (four roots of `delivery-root.json`, two of them competing for seat 1's receipt slot, repeated and with one byte changed): every invariant holds for the root event, and the fourth holds with its one exception, a root refusing exactly the other roots of its seat and kind, as `root-repeat`. Its forced-path test measures a root call with dense calldata cold against real registries, under `ROOT_CALL_GAS` and above 80% of it, and the seat's other root with exactly that limit ([[delivery]], Capacity).

`test/BoardAvailability.t.sol` runs every record of `vectors/availability.json` through `submitAvailabilityRecord` against stubs set from the file's election and default seats: each record naming no root is accepted and emitted with its definition hash and seat as topics and the data `vectors/contract-event-v8.json` records for it, a repeat emitting nothing, those naming roots the board doesn't hold are refused `availability-root`, and the author panel's, accepted against an author key set of the seats that signed them, `availability-seat` without one; every decode rejection with its code at its offset (a type [[versioning]] lists `unexpected-record-type`, where the file, which knows only the two types, says unknown), with types 0, `0x001a`, `0x001d` and `0xff00`, version 0 and a record without its version; the refusal order (a stray definition before its seat, seats 0, 5 and 255, every author seat without an author panel, an author seat outside the author key set and one under the default seat's key of its index, another seat's key, each type's tag over the other's record, the honest record accepted after); the slots (a forged record filling nothing, a second attestation of a point with another checkpoint refused while the first's repeat is the no-op, the complaint and the other point in their own slots, a fifth record refused, other seats and another election keeping theirs); the roots, against `vectors/delivery-root.json`'s election (its `recordHash` named only once the board holds the root, by another seat as well as its own, two seats' roots together, a root the board doesn't hold, a zero word and another election's root refused, a refused attestation filling nothing), and the root slots keeping the record hash, a second root of the kind still `root-repeat`; canonical calldata; and fuzzing of any signed checkpoint and CID accepted, of arbitrary records and of one-byte changes to an honest one, each refused with the check's own errors. Under `osaka`, where P-256 goes through its precompile, it measures each call cold with its intrinsic gas: a complaint about 172,000, an attestation naming no root about 151,000, and one naming the 32 roots of sixteen seats about 422,000 in 1,252 bytes of calldata, each well within one L1 deposit, so a seat can force its record through L1 by its cutoff ([[availability]], Windows).

`test/BoardCloseRule.t.sol` replays `vectors/contract-event-v3.json`'s board sequence (an ordinary ballot, relayed ballots of versions 1 and 2, one relayed twice after its ordinary submission, at the call fields' extremes and with a ballot the verifier rejects at a validity proof, and four close statements) through the board placed at the file's address on its chain, each call made from the record's own event data at its L2 block and time, and requires each log's topics and data, and the accumulator after it, to be the file's. It runs `vectors/close-statement.json`'s window verdicts for the ordinary path by the block's time and the relayed path by the stamp alone, with the block hours after close; `vectors/relay.json`'s L2 aliases, each accepted from its alias and refused from its L1 address; every statement of `close-statement.json` with its event, and every decode rejection with the board's codes (a listed type `unexpected-record-type`, any version but 1 `unknown-version`, where the file decodes against a pin), and `r` at 0 and `n`, `s` at 0 and above the low half, the low half's top (framed, then `statement-signature`), an unlisted type and type 0. Each refusal code and the checking order have tests: `relay-sender` before calldata for four other senders, canonical calldata with a word above 2⁶⁴, `relay-window` and `ballot-window` at both edges and by fuzzing, the ballot check's codes on the relayed path, refusals not remembered, the relayed path's emitting for every accepted deposit and an ordinary copy after it a no-op, `statement-stray`, `statement-seat` for five seats outside the key set, `statement-signature` for another seat's key and each signed field changed, and `statement-limit` (the same claim with other bytes, a third claim, repeats before and after the limit, another seat and another election keeping their own). Fuzz tests: no arbitrary statement, statement body or one-byte change to an honest one is accepted, and every refusal is one of the board's errors; no sender but the alias is accepted. An invariant suite (`test/invariant/BoardCloseRule.invariant.t.sol`) interleaves ordinary and relayed ballots at times and stamps around the window's edges, deposits from the alias and from others, and statements of four seats with three claims against two L1 blocks, and holds that an ordinary ballot is accepted exactly inside the window unless the board holds its bytes, a relayed deposit exactly from the alias with its stamp inside whatever the board holds, each seat at most two statements per election with different claims and none within that limit refused, and the accumulator the replay of exactly the logs emitted (T-35, T-50, T-71, T-17).

`test/BoardRollingBallot.t.sol` runs every ballot of `vectors/ballot-v5-records.json` through `submitRollingBallot` with the root `vectors/contract-event-v10.json` supplies for it, against stubs recording each rolling cell with the root 0: each accepted ballot's log is that file's record, and the board's verdict differs from the verifier's admission only at the validity proofs and `root-step-range` (Rolling ballots). It also runs the file's root mismatch (accepted) and front-run, 1, `r − 1`, 0 and `r` roots (refused), a fuzz over roots and one over one-byte changes on either path, the file's decode rejections, each path's version, the window and canonical calldata. `test/invariant/Board.invariant.t.sol` adds the rolling ballot with its own, the front-run or any root, changed by one byte, and on the ordinary path, to the board's invariants: a refused copy isn't remembered, its own root still passes after any sequence, a repeat with any root emits nothing, and each `RollingBallotSubmitted` re-passes the rolling check with its root.

**Gas** (measured from the caller under `cancun`, where OpenZeppelin's `P256` falls back to Solidity, including calldata, the record's storage key and the accumulator step): a ballot of three options at `k = 2` and `n = 3` about 405,000 (about 12,000 of it the call to `BallotCheck` and the author key set's read, Deployment, The ballot check); one share receipt about 343,000 and three in one `submitReceipts` call about 950,000; a share-receipt batch of 1,024 pairs, where P-256 goes through its precompile, about 4,172,000 cold; a complaint about 342,000; a posting at three options, with its ballot's check, about 434,000 (the tests mock the pairing for postings, since no version-1 vector pairs a real membership proof with ciphertexts, and add its measured cost, about 183,000); under ballot version 4, measured from the call without calldata, a version-2 receipt about 352,000 for a default seat and 367,000 for an author seat (whose key set and signing key the election registry holds), a complaint about 345,000, and a posting at nine options with its ballot's check and a real membership proof about 544,000, a version-2 batch of two pairs, of either kind, about 346,000 to 376,000 (107,000 to 136,000 where P-256 goes through its precompile), an author seat's full receipt batch of 1,024 pairs about 2,780,000 through the precompile, and a version-2 root about 370,000 to 376,000 (128,000 to 136,000 through the precompile), each figure the first of its kind on a board, so a default seat's call, whose key set the trustee registry holds, is the dearer; a byte-identical repeat about 29,000; a relayed ballot of three options about an ordinary one's from the alias; a close statement about 369,000, or about 128,000 where P-256 goes through its precompile; an obligation root, where P-256 goes through its precompile, about 176,000 cold against real registries with its intrinsic gas, whatever the number of leaves it commits to, its record hash, which its slot keeps, included; an availability attestation or complaint, where P-256 goes through its precompile, about 151,000 to 172,000 cold with its intrinsic gas, and an attestation naming 32 roots about 422,000. The largest version-3 record ([[ballot-validity]], Committed vote (version 3): `T = MAX_OPTIONS` options at `k = n = MAX_TRUSTEES`, and `MAX_QUESTIONS` two-option questions of range 0 to 2, whose 96 range branches are the most `T + Q` allows) is 48,380 bytes, 48,452 of calldata, under op-geth's 128 KiB transaction size; with dense bytes, as an honest ballot's are, the board frames, checks and emits it for about 2.7 million gas including its calldata at the standard price, with the pairing mocked, and the transaction, 21,000 plus the larger of that with the pairing and its calldata at EIP-7623's floor price, costs about 2.9 million, far below EIP-7825's per-transaction cap of 2²⁴; the test's slots are warm, so a real call pays some tens of thousands more for cold reads (T-25, T-52). On an L2 with the `P256VERIFY` precompile a receipt or complaint costs about 120,000 alone (a version-2 one from the call about 107,000 to 127,000), and under `osaka`, cold and against real registries as a forced deposit runs, a call of 256 receipts about 17,160,000 with its intrinsic gas, about 67,000 a receipt, and a complaint call about 157,000: the figures [[delivery]]'s capacity check sizes trustee nodes' obligations by (Capacity).

### Known-weak

- **A pairing repriced above 600,000 gas** for four pairs would make every proof fail as `non-canonical`, and the immutable board would need replacing. `ecPairing` has only been repriced downwards (EIP-1108), but other precompiles have gone up (EIP-7883 raised `ModExp`), which is why the cap is over three times today's price.
- **What a member's direct submission can still carry.** Through the contract path the archive keeps records the verifier rejects, so the fields the ballot check can't judge are free to a member with a valid membership proof: the L1 reference (40 bytes), each validity-proof scalar below `ℓ` (about 252 bits, 64 bytes per option and per branch), and the VSS commitments as any even values below `2²⁵⁵ − 19` (about 254 bits each, `32 · k · T` bytes), against random-looking bytes on the board path (T-17; [[ballot-validity]], Residuals). In a rolling poll anyone, member or not, can archive the same fields through the rolling call at gas cost, since the board checks the proof against a root its submitter supplies (Rolling ballots, What a submitter controls).
- **`qualified` is an input** to the library: the board MUST take it from the trustee registry's `keySetOf`, never from a submitter, or a submitter could name the seats whose share hashes it fills.
- **Contract-path receipts and complaints carry their seat's chosen fields.** The board path refuses a receipt for a ballot not on the board, or whose `share_hash` isn't the ballot's ([[delivery]], Boarding, rules 2 and 4); the contract can't see the board, so a registered seat can archive a receipt with any `ballot_hash` and `share_hash` (64 chosen bytes), or a complaint with any `ballot_hash` and an unchecked `D`, as often as it pays for, each attributed to its seat by its signature (T-17, T-52). The delivery check ignores every such record.
- **Anyone who saw a ciphertext can post junk.** A posting's `m` is free (32 bytes), and the board accepts any number of postings per seat and ballot, so whoever saw `ct_i` and its ballot (in the voter's pending transaction, or on the board) can archive 32 chosen bytes per posting, each with about 1.5 to 5.4 KB of calldata and a ballot check to pay for (T-17, T-52). Refusing them would let a junk posting occupy the voter's slot ([[delivery]], Direct submit), so the verifier ignores them instead (`posting-encapsulation`).
- **A member can post chosen ciphertexts, and in a rolling poll anyone can.** A member can make up a ballot that passes the board's check (its validity proofs and elements unchecked, Ballots, What it leaves out) whose share hashes commit to ciphertexts of its choosing, never submit it, and post each: `n` ciphertexts of about 1.2 to 5.4 KB of chosen bytes per ballot made up, any number of ballots, each costing a membership proof's check and the postings' gas (T-17, T-52). The archive keeps them, and the delivery check ignores postings for a ballot not on the board. Requiring the ballot to be on the board first would need the contract to see the board, which it can't, or every posting's ballot to be direct-submitted as well, which archives it twice; the member can already put about as many chosen bytes in its own ballots (the item before). In a rolling poll the ballot's proof is checked against a root its sender supplies, so anyone, member or not, can do the same through `submitRollingPosting` at gas cost, as through the rolling call (Delivery records (ballot version 5), What a sender controls).
- **Nothing bounds a call's batch or count but gas.** A trustee node's receipts and complaints are bounded by what it owes, and it serves only elections whose obligation fits the forced path ([[delivery]], What a node owes, Capacity); everything else on the contract path, a member's re-votes included, is bounded only by what its sender pays, as [[delivery]] accepts. Every accepted record is archived and replayed by every verifier of every election through the board (The shared event sequence above; T-52).
- **The board's events are archived whole only under contract-event version 8, or version 9 for a board that accepts ballot version 4 or the version-2 delivery records.** Version 1 names none, version 2 neither of the close rule's, version 3 not the batches', version 4 not the root's and versions 5 to 7 not the availability events (Contract-event record, Event tables); the election registry refuses a profile pinning versions 1 to 7, version 5 for want of the group registry's Tier 1 event, version 6 of its self-paid events and version 7 of the availability events (Election registry, Registration, rule 9), and pins version 8 since [[P4-3]] part 2c-3; a board deployed beside another registry depends on that registry refusing them too. A board that accepts ballot version 4 and the version-2 delivery records needs version 9, which frames them (Contract-event record, Event tables (version 9)), and the registry deployed with it must refuse version 8 as well ([[P2-16]] part 5e). A board with the rolling calls needs version 11, and the registry deployed with it must refuse versions 9 and 10 as well ([[P2-17]] part 5).
- **A forced rolling ballot's root is chosen by its caller.** The relay's rolling call records whatever root word it is given, and the board checks the proof against it, so anyone can force, at L1 gas, a ballot made against a tree of their own, as on the ordinary rolling call (Rolling ballots, What a submitter controls); the verifier rejects it (`root-step-mismatch`) and it counts nothing (T-52, T-53). A rolling call the board refuses (a root of 0 or at or above `r`, or one the proof isn't against) leaves its 32-byte root word, with its record, in L1 calldata and state for good, as any refused relay call does (L1 relay, Known-weak, Anyone can record calls; T-17).
- **An availability record's L1 fields are free to its seat.** The contract can't read L1, so a registered seat can archive, per election, two attestations and two complaints whose `checkpoint_hash` and complaint CID digest it chooses (about 32 chosen bytes each), attributed by its signature; the slots bound it (Availability records; T-17). [[availability]]'s check reports an attestation naming another checkpoint against its seat (`availability-checkpoint`).
- **A batch's pairs are free to its seat.** As with single receipts, the contract can't see the board, so a registered seat can archive up to 1,024 pairs of chosen ballot and share hashes per batch (64 chosen bytes per pair, up to 65,536 bytes of pair data per call, about 4,100 gas a pair on the forced path's figures), as often as it pays for, each batch attributed to its seat by its signature (T-17, T-52). The delivery check ignores every pair that names no selected ballot, and one whose share hash isn't the ballot's is reported against the seat.
- **A root's fields are free to its seat.** The contract can't fetch or judge the leaves, so a registered seat can archive, per election, one receipt root and one complaint root of any `root`, any `count` and any digest in `leaves_cid` (about 68 chosen bytes each, 136 per seat and election, each attributed by its signature); the slot bounds it, unlike batches (T-17). The delivery check reads a root's leaves only if the availability committee names it and they match it, ignores a root above the obligation's bound before fetching anything, and reports a wrong one against its seat ([[delivery]], Leaf availability, Delivery check; T-52).
- **Anyone can archive copies of a ballot through the relay.** The relayed path keeps no repeats, so whoever has seen a ballot record (on the board, or in a pending transaction) can relay it again, by anyone's L1 call, and each accepted deposit is one more event every verifier replays and 4.5 matches against an L1 storage proof; on the other paths only a member's own re-votes add records. Each copy costs its caller an L1 call and the portal's metered deposit gas, and the portal's deposit budget bounds how many land per L1 block (T-52, T-17; P4-2 measures the price). Each relay call's gas limit (a `uint64` the caller chooses, between the portal's minimum and its maximum, about 24 bits) and the block it lands in are archived with it: a few chosen bits per call (T-17). Refusing repeats instead would turn a relayed copy of an ordinary ballot into a no-op (Relayed ballots, No repeats on this path).
- **The window is the block's time.** The sequencer still chooses which transactions fill the blocks up to `closes_at`, and can rebuild unposted ones until `L_δ`; the close check (6.3) detects a rebuild past δ, and the board can't ([[close]], Residuals; T-35, T-45). A ballot whose transaction misses the last block at or before `closes_at` is refused, never boarded late, on this path as on the relayed one, so a client submits well before close and relays through L1 when the sequencer censors it (T-50).
- **The board's size.** With the availability call its runtime code was 24,484 bytes of EIP-170's 24,576; with the ballot check in `BallotCheck` (Deployment, The ballot check) and ballot version 4 it was 18,734 and `BallotCheck` 9,164; with the version-2 receipts, postings and complaints ([[P2-16]] part 5c-3) it was 19,820, and with the version-2 batches and roots and the availability call's author branch (part 5c-4) it is 20,484, about 4 KB below the limit, before more code moves out (the delivery checks into a contract of their own, the same way). `forge build --sizes` fails the package's check if either grows past the limit.
- **A second contract to verify.** [[verifier]] 1.2 must match `BallotCheck`'s code as well as the board's and tie its address to the board's first creation (Deployment, The ballot check); a 1.2 that matched only the board would let a board whose constructor ran other code, or whose `ballotCheck` word named another contract, pass. Until 1.2 is implemented it is `not-implemented` for every contract alike (T-64).
- **The other calls read types up to `0x001a`.** The ballot, delivery and close-statement checks call a type above `0x001a` `unknown-record-type`, the two availability types included, where [[versioning]] lists them: those checks' vectors were written before them, and only the code differs, never a verdict.
- **Soundness rests on the L2's precompiles**: a bug in its `ecPairing`, or one that skips G2's subgroup check, would admit forged proofs on chain, though never past the verifier, which checks every proof with `@noble/curves` ([[membership]], Verification; T-05, T-71). The vectors include a `B` outside the subgroup, so a test run against an EVM that skips the check fails.

## Board anchor

Threats: T-23 (board tampering), T-24 (equivocation), T-52 (front-running an election's anchor), T-53 (discreditation), T-49 and T-50 (outages and censorship that miss a deadline, and finding the archive once our domain and hosting are gone), T-51 (data loss), T-17 (chosen bytes on L1), T-69 (a compelled or seized anchoring key), T-26 (unprovable drops: receipt keys fixed before voting).

The board anchor is our first contract on L1. It records, once per election and segment, the hash of the board's checkpoint after that segment ([[board]], Segments and checkpoints), and publishes the checkpoint itself in the same log, so every checkpoint is attributable to its election and segment from L1 alone. Each call may also carry the `archiveLocator` [[0005-permanent-archive]] requires (Archive objects and how they are found), so the archive is found from L1 alone. It also keeps the record of receipt keys that [[0023-receipt-evidence]] requires (Receipt keys, below), so the keys that judge an election's receipts are the same authority's. It is immutable: no owner, proxy, pause or upgrade path. Its clock and its anchoring authority are fixed at deployment as immutables, which solc writes into the runtime code. A verifier release pins the anchor's address together with its `epoch`, `period`, `deadline` and `authority`; [[verifier]] 1.2 compares the runtime code with the release build with the immutables' byte ranges masked, and requires the masked words to equal those pinned values, so the clock a verifier applies is the deployed one, read from code, never from a call. The definition's `l1_anchor` names it.

### Deployment

The anchor is deployed with the anchoring clock of [[board]], Timing, and the address of the anchoring authority, all immutable:

| Value       | Deployed value            | Meaning                                                                                                                                                                           |
| ----------- | ------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `epoch`     | a whole hour, in L1 time  | The start of period 0. The L2 registry carries the same value (Election registry, Deployment).                                                                                    |
| `period`    | 3,600 seconds             | Hourly anchoring, as the brief sets.                                                                                                                                              |
| `deadline`  | 43,200 seconds (12 hours) | How long after a period ends its checkpoint may still be anchored.                                                                                                                |
| `authority` | our Safe on L1            | The only account that can anchor. Version-1 definitions bind no authority of their own ([[election-definition]], The seal), so this is the authority [[board]], Anchoring, means. |

Deployment reverts if `period` is 0, or if `authority` has no code or only an [EIP-7702](https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-7702) delegation (`0xef0100 ‖ address`): both are a single key. Code doesn't prove a multisig (a 1-of-1 Safe passes), so the deployment record (`packages/contracts/deployments/<chain-id>.json`) names the Safe, its owners and its threshold at deployment. A Safe's owners and threshold can change afterwards; nothing here pins them over time (Known-weak). Because the clock is in the anchor, never in a definition, no one can choose a deadline after seeing a board (T-24, T-53).

**Why 12 hours.** [[board]], Residuals, asks P4-1 to justify the deadline against two costs. A missed deadline fails the election permanently (T-49, T-50, T-53): 12 hours covers an overnight outage of the anchoring service before anyone is paged, a fee spike that prices out a few hourly attempts, a Safe signer offline for part of a day, and L1 censorship far longer than Ethereum's observed inclusion delays, with twelve hourly retries from independent runners inside the window. It assumes the custody P4-1 part 2 sets up: hourly anchoring is unattended, so each runner holds a signer key online, and the Safe's threshold is met by runners on independent hosting (T-69, Known-weak). The fork window it opens is bounded the other way: a voter's client can check a receipt against its segment's checkpoint as soon as it is anchored (the anchor admits no second one), and completes [[board]] client rule 2, which also needs every earlier segment's checkpoint, after its segment's deadline, at most `period + deadline` (13 hours) after casting; and a closed election is verifiable at most 12 hours after its seal's period ends. Elections run for days, so a same-day confirmation is enough, and anchoring runs hourly regardless: the deadline is the bound on a failure, not the normal delay. A shorter deadline would make one bad night fail an election; a longer one would let a voter's receipt stay unconfirmed into the next day.

### Anchoring

`anchor(bytes checkpoints, bytes locator)` takes zero or more checkpoints, each `encode(checkpoint)` of [[board]] (checkpoint version 2, 180 bytes: `election_id ‖ u32(segment) ‖ u64(size) ‖ head ‖ root ‖ object_cid ‖ hashes_cid`; [[0029-board-data-availability]]), concatenated, so one transaction can carry several elections' checkpoints, and an archive locator, possibly empty (Archive locator, below). The call reverts, anchoring nothing, unless:

1. the caller is `authority` (T-52: no one else can front-run an election's checkpoint with a bogus one);
2. the calldata is exactly the ABI encoding of the two `bytes` arguments (offsets `0x40` and `0x60 + pad32(len(checkpoints))`, tails in that order, zero padding, nothing after the second), the call carries a checkpoint or a locator, `checkpoints` is a multiple of 180 bytes, every checkpoint's `object_cid` and `hashes_cid` start `0x01 ‖ codec ‖ 0x12 ‖ 0x20` with `codec` `0x55` (raw) or `0x70` (dag-pb), the only CIDs [[content-addressing]] makes, so that of each CID only its 32-byte digest is the authority's choice (whether the digest names the right bytes needs the board, so that is the verifier's check, `checkpoint-cid` in [[board]], Board checks), and a non-empty `locator` is well framed (T-17);
3. every checkpoint has `size ≥ 1`: an empty board's checkpoint fails the election (`checkpoint-size`), so it is never stored;
4. for every checkpoint, `end ≤ block.timestamp ≤ end + deadline`, where `end = epoch + (segment + 1)·period` is the end of its period ([[board]], Timing);
5. no checkpoint has been stored for its election and segment, in an earlier call or earlier in this one, even an equal one. Refusing an equal one too keeps the rule a plain write-once; the job that anchors (P4-1 part 2) reads the slots first, so a batch never carries a checkpoint another runner already anchored.

For each checkpoint, in order, it stores `checkpoint_hash = H(DS("abolish/v1/board-checkpoint-2", encode(checkpoint)))` for `(election_id, segment)`, lowers the election's lowest anchored segment if `segment` is below it (or sets it, for the election's first anchor), and emits

| Event                                                                                                                                                                               | Topics                                                        | Data                                                                                          |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `CheckpointAnchored(bytes32 indexed electionId, uint32 indexed segment, bytes32 indexed checkpointHash, uint64 size, bytes32 head, bytes32 root, bytes objectCid, bytes hashesCid)` | `election_id`, `segment` as a 32-byte word, `checkpoint_hash` | `size`, `head`, `root`, `object_cid`, `hashes_cid`, ABI-encoded (each CID as 36-byte `bytes`) |

through the event accumulator (above), which the anchor inherits like every Abolish contract, with `chain_id` the L1's. After the checkpoints, a non-empty locator is logged as

| Event                                                   | Topics                      | Data                                      |
| ------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------- | ----------------------------------------- |
| `ArchiveLocated(uint16 indexed version, bytes locator)` | `version` as a 32-byte word | the whole locator, ABI-encoded as `bytes` |

### Archive locator

[[0005-permanent-archive]] has every L1 anchor carry a generic, versioned `archiveLocator` naming the latest confirmed archive manifest, with no storage scheme fixed in the contract, so the archive can change without a redeploy. The anchor frames it and doesn't interpret it: `locator = u16(version) ‖ body`, with `version ≠ 0` (so a zero word never reads as a locator), a body of at least 1 byte, and at most `MAX_LOCATOR_LENGTH = 128` bytes in all, which bounds the authority's chosen bytes per locator, not in total (T-17, Known-weak). Each version's body is specified with the manifest, as a fixed length per version, with vectors: version 1, 70 bytes in all, names an Arweave manifest ([[archive]], Locator). A verifier ignores versions it doesn't know, reporting them ([[verifier]] 4.3), and reports a locator of the wrong length for its version.

A call may carry a locator alone, with no checkpoint, at any time: the locator isn't tied to a period. Two readings of [[0005-permanent-archive]] follow, listed under Decisions to review in STATUS:

- Its "final anchor", which waits until every manifest holding a closed election's CARs is confirmed, is a locator-only call made whenever the archive confirms. The seal's checkpoint doesn't wait for it, so a slow archive can't push the seal past its deadline, and the deadline justification above is unchanged.
- "Each L1 anchor carries" a locator: the contract allows a call without one (there is none before the first manifest confirms), but the job that anchors (P4-1 part 2) MUST include the latest confirmed locator in every call that carries checkpoints once one exists, so no anchored period lacks a locator at or after it. Two exceptions, each reported by the job, since a missed window fails an election while a period anchored without a locator is still found from a later call's (T-49): when the archive's locator can't be read, and when the anchor holds a locator that doesn't name the archive's own chain at or below it, which the job never overwrites so its locators never fall (T-69; [[archive]], Locator).

The anchor also keeps the last locator and the number posted in state, so the archive is found from a storage proof alone, even once the logs that carried locators have expired from L1 history ([EIP-4444](https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-4444)):

- the last locator is a Solidity `bytes` at slot `A + 2`: for a locator of `len ≤ 31` bytes the slot holds the bytes left-aligned with `2·len` in its lowest byte; for a longer one it holds `2·len + 1`, and the bytes start at slot `keccak256(abi.encode(A + 2))`, 32 per slot;
- the count is the word at `A + 3`.

A verifier proves both at `B`, follows the last locator to its manifest, and from there walks the manifest chain back ([[0005-permanent-archive]]), which names every confirmed manifest. Every earlier locator is in an `ArchiveLocated` log; while L1 history serves them, a verifier that wants them all replays the anchor's logs against its event accumulator ([[contracts]], Event accumulator, Verification), which covers every checkpoint of every election as well, about 100 logs an hour at 100 live elections, so the latest locator from state is the path that scales.

### Receipt keys

[[0023-receipt-evidence]] has the anchoring authority register every receipt key on L1 before any election lists it, and retire it there, so the keys that judge an election's receipts ([[election-definition]], Receipt keys) are fixed by L1 records only that authority writes, and evidence under them is attributed to it alone (T-26). The anchor keeps the record itself, so the writer is the deployment's immutable `authority` with no second contract, address or verifier pin. L2 governance can forge any sender on the L2, which is why the record is on L1 (T-71, [[0004-l2-choice]]).

`registerReceiptKey(bytes key)` and `retireReceiptKey(bytes key)` each revert unless:

1. the caller is `authority`;
2. the calldata is exactly the ABI encoding of one `bytes` argument of 33 bytes: the selector, offset `0x20`, length 33, the 33 bytes, 31 zero bytes, nothing after (T-17);
3. `key[0]` is `0x02` or `0x03`.

The contract doesn't check that `x` is on the curve, which would need a modular square root on chain: a definition can list only canonical keys ([[trustee-identity]], Identity key), so a registered non-key judges nothing, and only the authority can pay to register one. Then:

- `registerReceiptKey` reverts if the key has ever been registered, retired or not, or if its twin has: the key with the other prefix and the same `x`, which is its negation, whose secret key anyone holding the key's own also holds (so retiring a seized key also keeps its negation out); otherwise it sets the key's `registered_at` to `block.timestamp`.
- `retireReceiptKey` reverts unless the key is registered and not retired; otherwise it sets its `retired_at` to `block.timestamp`.

Nothing else writes either field and neither is ever cleared, so the record is append-only: a key is registered once, retired at most once, and never re-registered (T-69). Each emits, through the event accumulator,

| Event                                                   | Topics                       | Data                             |
| ------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------- | -------------------------------- |
| `ReceiptKeyRegistered(bytes32 indexed x, uint8 prefix)` | `x`, the key's last 32 bytes | `prefix` (`key[0]`), ABI-encoded |
| `ReceiptKeyRetired(bytes32 indexed x, uint8 prefix)`    | `x`                          | `prefix`                         |

The record of key `p ‖ x` is the word at `keccak256(abi.encode(x, keccak256(abi.encode(uint256(p), A + 4))))`: `registered_at` in its low 64 bits, `retired_at` in the next 64, the rest zero (Solidity's `mapping(uint8 => mapping(bytes32 => Record))` at `A + 4`, with `struct Record { uint64 registeredAt; uint64 retiredAt; }`). Zero means never registered, or not retired. A word with any of its high 128 bits set, with `retired_at` set and `registered_at` zero or above it, or with either time after the timestamp of the block it is proven at (the anchor writes `block.timestamp`), can't be written by the release code, so [[verifier]] 2.9 fails the election on it (`receipt-key-record`) rather than reading either field from it, and the client refuses to cast. A verifier proves each listed key's word with `eth_getProof` at its finalized L1 block `B`, as it proves anchors (Verification, below), never from an `eth_call` or a list of logs, and applies [[election-definition]]'s judging rule ([[verifier]] 2.9); the ballot client reads the same words before casting. The words stay in state, so they survive L1 history expiry as the anchors do.

Custody and use:

- The receipt keys themselves sit in the board server's key service ([[receipts]], Residuals); the authority's quorum only registers and retires them, so it holds no receipt key.
- The authority registers a key well before any election lists it, and retires it as soon as a leak is suspected, and every key when it rotates it, so a seized key can't be listed in a later election by a hostile creator ([[0023-receipt-evidence]]). A retirement after an election's `opens_at` changes nothing for it.
- A registration costs about 65,000 gas, its transaction's base cost included (a new storage word, the accumulator and a log), once per key, never per election.

The anchor doesn't know an election's seal ([[board]], Timing): it is computed from the definition, which lives on L2. A verifier ignores an anchor for a segment after the seal. Nor does it check that an election's checkpoints grow with their segments: with a 12-hour deadline, a later segment can be anchored before an earlier one, and [[board]], Board checks, catches an inconsistent pair from L1 alone (`checkpoint-inconsistent`).

### Verification

Rule 5 means L1 never holds two checkpoints for one election and segment, and rules 1 and 4 mean every stored one counts: it came from the authority, inside its window. So a verifier reads an election's anchors completely from state, never from a list of logs an RPC node could shorten (T-24):

- The hash for `(election_id, s)` is the word at `keccak256(abi.encode(uint256(s), keccak256(abi.encode(election_id, A))))`, where `A` is the [ERC-7201](https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-7201) namespace `abolish.board-anchor`:

  ```
  A = keccak256(abi.encode(uint256(keccak256("abolish.board-anchor")) − 1)) & ~0xff
    = 0x16eba4501d0a9dddbfa03518d843c364be664629e6d99efb7169d8f090095b00
  ```

  Zero means none was anchored. The election's **lowest anchored segment** plus one (0 if it has no anchor) is the word at `keccak256(abi.encode(election_id, A + 1))`. An election's board carries entries from before voting opens (the ceremony transcript, the contract events of its creation and registration), so no bound derived from `opens_at` would do. The election's **first segment** `f` is the period containing `l1OriginTimestamp` of its `ElectionCreated` event, the L1 origin of the L2 block holding it, which is on its board and authenticated by the registry's accumulator, and which the verifier checks against finalized L1 and the event's L2 timestamp (Election registry, Events, The L1 origin; [[verifier]] 4.4). Every entry of an honest board is appended after creation, so none has a segment below `f`. The verifier proves the lowest-segment word, then the word for every segment from `max(lowest, f)` to the seal, all with `eth_getProof` against the state root of one finalized L1 block `B` (how it trusts L1 headers is [[verifier]]'s), and never trusts an `eth_call` answer. `B`'s timestamp is the `now` of [[board]], Board checks: proving at one block and taking `now` from a later one would read a segment anchored in between as `unanchored`, or miss a checkpoint anchored there, so two verifiers could disagree (T-36).

  An anchor for a segment below `f` doesn't count for the election: it isn't passed to [[board]], Board checks, and an entry with a segment below `f` is in no counted checkpoint's segment, so it fails `unanchored` once its window has closed. A segment from `f` up and below the lowest anchored one has no anchor, so its entries fail `unanchored` the same way. The verifier proves the `lowest` word only to take that maximum; the range is then as long as the election's board is, in hours.

  **Pre-creation anchors.** Election identifiers are predictable (`electionIdAt` is a public view of the registry's counter), so whoever holds the anchoring quorum at some moment (a compelled or seized one, T-69) could anchor checkpoints with random heads and roots for elections not yet created. Rule 5 would keep them, and once those elections run, a verifier would find a word no board matches and fail them (`checkpoint-inconsistent`): one short compulsion failing elections that didn't exist yet. Two rules stop that:

  - The `f` rule above ignores every anchor for a segment that ended before the election's L1 origin, which covers any anchor made before the creation's L1 origin block.
  - That leaves anchors made between the origin block and the creation itself, at most the L2's `max_sequencer_drift` (1,800 seconds on Base, [[0004-l2-choice]]) plus a period boundary. So a creator MUST, after its `ElectionCreated` event is final ([[contracts]], Election registry, Election identifier) and before it asks the panel for a ceremony, prove at a finalized L1 block `C` whose time is after the creation's L1 origin plus `max_sequencer_drift` that the checkpoint word is zero for every segment from `f` to the last period that ended before `C` (a handful of slots: no later segment can have been anchored by then), or, if any of them is set, abandon the identifier and create another. It checks those slots, never `lowestSegmentPlusOne`: anchors below `f` don't count, and a quorum held briefly could anchor the current segment for thousands of upcoming identifiers in one transaction, so refusing any identifier with an anchor at all would let one compulsion block every future election. An abandoned identifier gets no definition, so it holds no result. After that check, an anchor no board matches can only be made while the election exists, the residual [[board]] already states (attributable, and only while it runs).

- For each non-zero word it needs the checkpoint itself. A word equal to the hash of the checkpoint a board copy gives at that segment's boundary needs nothing more: the contract stored it only inside its window, so its `anchoredAt` for [[board]], Board checks, may be taken as the end of its period, which gives the same result as the true time (timely, and at most `now`, since the word is proven at `B`). Only a word no copy matches needs the checkpoint itself, to name it and attribute the failure. Its hash is the word, so it is authenticated by the word whatever its source, typically the `CheckpointAnchored` log that wrote it; the word, proven at `B`, already attributes it to the authority inside its window, so no receipt proof is needed ([[verifier]], Anchors from L1). So old elections stay verifiable from state and the archive, without old L1 receipts, which ordinary nodes drop under history expiry ([EIP-4444](https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-4444)). A non-zero word that matches no checkpoint anyone can produce makes the election unverifiable ([[board]], Anchoring).

The anchor's own event accumulator gives the same completeness for its log sequence, but the per-segment words are what a verifier needs, and they need no archive replay.

### Vectors

The checkpoint hash and the window rule are the ones `vectors/board.json` already exercises: the Solidity tests hash every checkpoint there and compare it with the published `hash`, and anchor the `valid` board's checkpoints, with their CIDs, at their vector times, under the vectors' own clock.

### Known-weak

- **The anchoring authority can fail a running election** by anchoring a checkpoint no board matches, before the honest one (rule 5 then keeps it). That is attributable on L1, but nothing prevents it (T-24, T-53, T-69). A late anchor can't do it: the window closes 12 hours after each period, and no anchor lands before its period ends.
- **The quorum is held online.** Unattended hourly anchoring means the runners hold enough signer keys to meet the Safe's threshold, so seizing or compelling those runners (T-69) is seizing the quorum. The Safe protects against one leaked key and one rogue runner, not against whoever controls the runners' hosting.
- **The Safe's configuration can change after deployment**, and the anchor pins only its address. A quorum can reduce the owners or threshold, or make one key the authority without touching either: by enabling a module (which executes as the Safe with no signatures, a common way to automate jobs), setting a guard or fallback handler, or delegatecalling to replace the proxy's singleton. The Safe's getters don't show all of it: a delegatecall can write a module or an owner into its mappings without linking it into the lists `getModules` and `getOwners` walk, and the Safe lets that entry act. So the authority is a SafeL2, which logs every execution with its operation, and P4-1 part 2 MUST use no module, guard or delegatecall. Before every signature the anchoring runner checks the Safe against the deployment record at the block it signs at (P4-1 part 2b-2a): its history since deployment (set up with no setup delegatecall, never a delegatecall since, never a module's execution), and, which that history makes sound, its state (the proxy's and singleton's code, the singleton it delegates to, the owners, a threshold of at least 2, no module, no guard and the named fallback handler); it signs nothing for a Safe that differs. The runner runs the same check at startup, at the finalized block, and doesn't start for a Safe that differs (P4-1 part 2b-2b-1). That stops our own runners from serving a changed Safe; it doesn't stop the quorum that changed it. The verifier's check of the Safe (the same history and state at each anchoring block) is still to come; until then such a change is visible only in the Safe's own public transactions and in the runners' refusal.
- **Pre-creation anchors depend on the creator's check.** An anchor for a not-yet-created election in the drift window before its creation counts, and fails the election if no board matches it, unless the creator ran the per-slot check above and abandoned the identifier. A creator that skips the check can lose its election to an earlier compelled quorum; nothing on L1 enforces the check, and the anchor can't see L2. An anchor made after creation but before the check also forces a discard, which costs liveness only. A creator-chosen salt in `election_id` would make identifiers unpredictable before creation and remove the check; that is a registry change (P1-18), cheap while nothing is deployed.
- **Constructor effects aren't bound.** [[verifier]] 1.2 compares runtime code only, so a deployer's own initcode can do anything a constructor can and then return the release runtime code. That covers its whole effect: storage (here checkpoint words for predictable future elections or segments past the creator's check, `lowestSegmentPlusOne`, the latest archive locator, receipt-key words; in every contract, the accumulator's genesis and count) and every log it emits.
