Examples: a pre-written anchor word fails an election with the blame on an authority that never anchored; a constructor-emitted `DefinitionRegistered` for a predictable future `election_id` makes verifier 2.1 find two registrations and fail that election; a `PanelPinned` log whose kind isn't the stored one makes the logged kind say nothing about which civic-record definitions the election registry refuses (Trustee registry, Known-weak, The kind is the deployer's word; [[verifier]] 2.5 still fails each against its release's kind); a `PanelPinned` or `RegistrarsPinned` log naming the pinned keys while storage holds others lets the deployer block ceremony registrations or group additions, or record ones signed by its own keys, which the verifier refuses, failing every election that names that group (`group-history`) or stub (T-25). A pre-written receipt-key word is worse than a forged failure: it registers a key the authority never registered, so receipts under a deployer-held key would yield `unanchored`, `forked` or drop verdicts attributed to the authority; attributing receipt verdicts is sound only once 1.2 passes. Apart from that, none makes a false result verify: the verifier rechecks every signature- and hash-based rule itself (approvals against its pinned panels and registrars, definition and transcript hashes, timing). But it does forge failures: while 1.2 isn't a pass, a failure that rests on contract storage or constructor logs may be the deployment's doing, so a report naming such a failure must name the unbound deployment as a possible cause, and attributing it to an authority isn't sound until 1.2 passes. The creator's check, the accumulator's Verification argument and the registries' first-event rules hold only for the release constructor. The fix binds each deployment to the release creation code plus pinned constructor arguments, never to selected slots (a pinned CREATE2 factory with the address recomputed from both, or the creation transaction's input with inclusion evidence); until it is specified and checked, 1.2 is unverifiable, and it MUST be specified before anything but the staging deployment is deployed (Testnet deployments; T-64).
- **`f` rests on the creation's L1 origin.** `ElectionCreated` carries it, authenticated by the registry's accumulator, and the verifier checks that it names a finalized L1 block no more than `max_sequencer_drift` before the event's L2 timestamp (Election registry, Events, The L1 origin): a forged, lower `f` would let pre-creation anchors count. Within the drift a sequencer or a rewritten predeploy can still name an earlier origin, moving `f` down by at most `⌈D / period⌉` periods for the release's pinned drift `D` (one on Base), which the creator's per-slot check covers, since it starts from the same event's `f` (T-71, T-69).
- **Withheld tails and late forks** remain [[board]]'s residual until P4-3 part 2c: an authority that anchors a checkpoint extending every receipt with entries it never publishes makes the election unverifiable, not failed. Checkpoint version 2 names each segment's CIDs on L1 ([[0029-board-data-availability]]), so what was withheld is named, but only part 2c's availability attestations make withholding the ballot or counted extent fail the election (`unavailable`). The anchor only frames the CIDs; one that doesn't name its segment's bytes fails the election at the verifier (`checkpoint-cid`).
- **The authority can fail an election through its receipt keys.** Retiring a key a registered definition lists, at or before its `opens_at`, fails the election ([[verifier]] 2.9), as a bogus anchor does: attributable on L1, not prevented (T-53, T-69). A quorum seized for good can also register a key of its own, and a creator working with it can list that key and forge evidence against the authority; that authority can fail elections anyway ([[board]], Residuals).
- **Receipt keys aren't checked on the curve,** only framed: the authority can register 33 bytes of its choice per call, once per value (T-17). No definition can list one that isn't a key.
- **No checkpoint for a segment past the seal is refused on L1**: the anchor can't read the definition. Verifiers ignore it; it costs the authority gas and changes no result.
- **The authority chooses 180 bytes per checkpoint (of which the CIDs' 8 framing bytes are fixed) and up to 126 per locator**, with no limit on how many beyond gas (locator-only calls at any time, checkpoints for any identifier), that land on L1 for good, such as a checkpoint for an election that doesn't exist, or a locator body of a version no one defined (T-17). Only the authority can, and the deployment's Safe is accountable for it.
- **The latest locator in state can be overwritten** by the authority at any time, so a seized quorum can replace it with one naming a fake or empty archive. Earlier locators stay in `ArchiveLocated` logs while L1 history serves them, and the manifest chain names every earlier confirmed manifest from any genuine one; once the logs have expired, a verifier with no earlier locator or mirror is left with the overwritten one, which makes elections unverifiable, never verified (T-69, T-51).
- **A locator only names where the archive is.** A wrong or stale one can send verifiers nowhere, which makes an election unverifiable, never verified: everything found is checked against the anchored checkpoints. The manifest chain ([[0005-permanent-archive]]) gives a second path to every manifest.
- **Gas.** In the tests a call with one checkpoint costs about 89,000 gas, and each further checkpoint in the same call about 39,000: a storage write, a read of the lowest-segment word, two SHA-256 calls (the hash and the accumulator) and a log. The two CIDs of checkpoint version 2 cost about 5,000 of that per checkpoint (72 more bytes of calldata, and their framing, hashing and logging). A locator adds about 24,000 (its slots and log). An election's first anchor costs about 22,000 more, for its lowest-segment word. At 100 live elections, hourly, that is about 3.9 million gas an hour, a recurring L1 cost: on testnets it is free, and spending real funds on it is the owner's decision.
- **The deployment and the Safe don't exist yet** (P4-1 part 2, with a `ci:` deploy workflow for Sepolia). The Safe check and the sender exist (P4-1 part 2b-2a), the Safe being SafeL2 v1.4.1, signed for with no `safeTxGas`, base gas or gas price, so an anchor call the anchor refuses reverts the Safe transaction as a whole (`GS013`), leaving the Safe's nonce and the anchor unchanged; the worker schedules the run once configured (P4-1 part 2b-2b-1), with one runner holding the whole threshold, which it refuses to do on any L1 but Sepolia or a local Anvil. The co-signer on independent hosting, so that no one runner holds the quorum, and alerting are part 2b-2b-2. Until the anchor and its Safe are deployed, nothing is anchored and every board is unanchored.

## L1 relay

Threats: T-50 (the sequencer censoring direct submission), T-71 (L2 governance forging a deposit from the relay's address), T-35 and T-45 (a ballot made after close), T-25 (a forced ballot kept off the board), T-17 (chosen bytes), T-52 (oversized input). Decided by [[0030-close-rule]] (Recommendation, Forced ballots) and [[0004-l2-choice]] (Consequences, Close rule).

A voter whose direct submissions the sequencer censors forces its ballot through L1: it calls the relay, our second contract on L1, with its ballot record, and the relay records the call in L1 state, with the number and timestamp of the L1 block the call ran in, and deposits the record to the board through the L2's portal. The L2 must include the deposit within its sequencing window (12 hours, [[0004-l2-choice]], Forced inclusion), whatever the sequencer wants. On L2 the board accepts a relayed ballot only from the relay's aliased address, and the close rule judges it in the window by the L1 timestamp the relay recorded, its **stamp**, never by when the deposit reached L2 ([[close]], Window). A verifier matches each relayed ballot on the board with exactly the call recorded here (Verification), so a deposit that L2 governance forges from the aliased address matches no call and fails ([[verifier]] 4.5). The relay is immutable: no owner, proxy, pause or upgrade path, and it holds no funds. It inherits the event accumulator (above), with `chain_id` the L1's, like the board anchor. The definition's `l1_relay` names it, and the election registry pins it (Election registry, Deployment).

This page specifies the relay: its call, what it records, its deposit and its event. The board's side (the relayed-ballot call, its sender and window checks, and the event that carries the call's index, block, stamp and gas limit) is Board, Relayed ballots, and contract-event version 3 archives that event (Contract-event record, Event tables (version 3)). `BallotRelay` (`packages/contracts`) is the relay, built in part 4c-3. A rolling poll's ballot needs the root its proof was made against beside it, which the first call can't carry, so the relay has a second call, its rolling call, specified in [[P2-17]] part 2b-2c-2 and built in its part 5d-2a (Rolling ballots, below; Board, Relayed rolling ballots; contract-event version 11).

### Deployment

The relay is deployed with two immutables:

| Value    | Meaning                                                                                                                                                                             |
| -------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `portal` | The L2's deposit contract on L1 (`OptimismPortal`, the proxy's address), the canonical portal the verifier's release pins ([[verifier]], Pinned trust roots).                       |
| `board`  | The board's address on L2, which every deposit calls. Fixed before deployment from the deployer's nonces or by CREATE2, as the board's and the registries' are (Board, Deployment). |

Deployment reverts if `portal` has no code or `board` is zero, and, as every contract's, if `block.chainid` is 2⁶⁴ or more (Event accumulator, Values). [[verifier]] 1.2 masks both immutables and requires `portal` to equal the pinned portal and `board` the definition's `board` (T-64).

### Relaying

`relayBallot(uint64 gasLimit, bytes record) returns (uint64 index)` reverts, recording and depositing nothing, unless, in this order:

1. **Calldata** (`relay-calldata`): the calldata is exactly the ABI encoding of the two arguments: the selector, `gasLimit` as a word below 2⁶⁴, the offset `0x40`, the record's length, the record, zero padding to a whole word, and nothing after, so a call carries no byte its arguments don't define (T-17).
2. **Size** (`relay-too-large`): `record` is at most `MAX_RELAY_RECORD = 119,776` bytes ([[parameters]]), the longest whose deposit data (below: 196 bytes and the record padded to whole words) fits the portal's cap of 120,000 bytes. Checked before anything is read from the record (T-52).
3. **Header** (`relay-truncated`, `relay-record-type`): `record` is at least 35 bytes, a record type, a version and a `definition_hash`, and its type is `0x0002`, a ballot record ([[ballot-validity]], Ballot record).

The contract reverts with `NonCanonicalCalldata` for `relay-calldata` (or with no data where Solidity's decoder finds the fault first: a `gasLimit` word of 2⁶⁴ or more, a length past the calldata's end, another selector), and with `RelayTooLarge(length)`, `RelayTruncated(length)` and `RelayRecordType(type)` for the others. The relay reads nothing else: not the version, the election or any field after the definition hash. It can't read the definition, which lives on L2, so it can't judge the window, the version or the ballot; the board judges all of them, as it judges a direct submission (Board, Ballots), and a record it refuses leaves a recorded call with no event (Verification). The header check only keeps the event's topic meaningful and stops calls that can't be a ballot.

Then, with `i` the number of calls the relay recorded before this one, `b = block.number` and `τ = block.timestamp` (the call reverts if either is 2⁶⁴ or more, as the accumulator does), it:

1. stores call `i`'s **call hash**

   ```
   call_hash = H(DS("abolish/v1/relay-call",
                    u64(l1_chain_id) ‖ bytes[20](relay) ‖ u64(i) ‖ u64(b) ‖ u64(τ) ‖
                    u64(gasLimit) ‖ record))
   ```

   where `l1_chain_id` is `block.chainid` and `relay` the relay's own address, and sets its count to `i + 1`;

2. emits

   | Event                                                                                   | Topics                                        | Data                     |
   | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------- | ------------------------ |
   | `BallotRelayed(bytes32 indexed definitionHash, uint64 indexed index, bytes32 callHash)` | the record's `definition_hash`, `i` as a word | `call_hash`, ABI-encoded |

   through its event accumulator;

3. calls `portal.depositTransaction(board, 0, gasLimit, false, data)` with

   ```
   data = abi.encodeCall(submitRelayedBallot, (i, b, τ, gasLimit, record))
   ```

   the selector of `submitRelayedBallot(uint64,uint64,uint64,uint64,bytes)`, the words of `i`, `b`, `τ` and `gasLimit`, the offset `0xa0`, the record's length, the record and its zero padding: `196 + pad32(len(record))` bytes;

4. returns `i`.

The writes come before the external call, so a call re-entering through the portal would only record a call of its own after this one; a reverting deposit (a gas limit below the portal's minimum for the data's length, or above what its metering allows) reverts the whole call, so every recorded call made a deposit. The call is not payable and the deposit carries no value: the caller's own L1 transaction pays the portal's metering for the deposit's gas, so the relay never holds funds. On L2 the deposit's sender is the relay's alias,

```
alias(relay) = (relay + 0x1111000000000000000000000000000000001111) mod 2¹⁶⁰
```

which the portal applies to every contract caller, and the deposit runs as a call from it to `board` with `data` and `gasLimit` gas. The board's relayed-ballot call accepts only that sender (Board, Relayed ballots, `relay-sender`). It MUST emit its relayed-ballot event for every deposit it accepts, even for a record byte-identical to one the board already accepted by another call: Board, Calls, Repeats then makes a relayed copy of a ballot first submitted on the ordinary path a no-op, so a sequencer that copies a forced ballot's bytes from L1 calldata into an ordinary block before the deposit, and withholds that block's batch past `L_δ`, would leave the ballot with only a late ordinary copy, turning the forced path's guarantee into the ordinary path's (T-50, T-25; [[close]], Residuals). So the relayed-ballot call never consults the board's repeats (Board, Relayed ballots, No repeats on this path).

**Why the whole record.** The call hash binds every byte of the record, not its `ballot_hash`. The ballot hash leaves out the membership proof and the L1 reference ([[ballot-validity]], Ballot hash), so a forged deposit carrying the same ballot with another proof or reference would match a call that bound only the hash; binding the record means the only relayed event that matches a call is the record its caller sent (T-71, T-31). Hashing it costs the caller about 12 gas per 32 bytes.

**Why a word per call.** The relay also keeps the event accumulator every contract keeps, over its `BallotRelayed` logs, which binds each call's index, block and stamp. Matching an event against the accumulator alone would need every earlier call's preimage, and a call whose deposit failed on L2 has no event to supply it, so a verifier couldn't match any later call. With one word per index, each relayed ballot is matched by itself, whatever else the relay recorded.

**The gas limit is the caller's.** The board's check of the largest ballots costs a few million gas (Board, Gas), and its membership check needs about 620,000 available for the pairing whatever it spends (Board, Membership proofs), so a client gives a limit above what its ballot needs; the portal's own minimum for the data's length applies too. Too low a limit leaves the call recorded and the deposit reverted on L2, which costs only the caller (Verification). The call hash binds the limit, and the board's event carries it, so a verifier reporting a call with no event can say whether the caller gave the limit its ballot needed, and so tell a caller's mistake from a deposit suppressed on L2. The metering is also a price: the portal burns L1 gas in proportion to the deposit's gas limit and a deposit base fee that rises when deposits exceed their per-block target (a few million gas), so under contention, or a state actor filling the deposit market, a large forced ballot's burn plus its calldata can pass what one L1 transaction may spend ([EIP-7825](https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-7825)'s 2²⁴), and that ballot can't be forced at all until the price falls (T-50; P4-2 measures it). [[0004-l2-choice]]'s metered budget (20,000,000 gas per L1 block) bounds how many forced ballots one L1 block takes. Who pays, and through which address, is P2-8's and P4-2's; the paying address is linked to the ballot as any direct submission's is (T-11, T-13).

**Size.** Every ballot record this spec defines fits `MAX_RELAY_RECORD`: the largest version-3 record is 48,380 bytes (Board, Gas), and version 4's author half adds at most 33,544 bytes ([[ballot-validity]], Ballot record (version 4), Sizes), about 82 KB; version 5 adds its `step`. A ballot version whose largest record exceeds it can't be forced through this relay, so it MUST NOT be pinned by a definition naming this relay ([[0030-close-rule]], Consequences).

### Rolling ballots

`relayRollingBallot(uint64 gasLimit, bytes32 root, bytes record) returns (uint64 index)` forces a rolling poll's ballot through L1 with the root its membership proof was made against, which the board's check needs and no contract computes (Board, Rolling ballots; [[0028-rolling-electorates]], Direct submission; T-50). It is `relayBallot` with the root beside the record, and differs only where the root does. It reverts, recording and depositing nothing, unless, in this order:

1. **Calldata** (`relay-calldata`): exactly the ABI encoding of the three arguments: the selector, `gasLimit` as a word below 2⁶⁴, the root's word (any 32 bytes), the offset `0x60`, the record's length, the record, zero padding to a whole word, and nothing after (T-17).
2. **Size** (`relay-too-large`): `record` is at most `MAX_ROLLING_RELAY_RECORD = 119,744` bytes ([[parameters]]), the longest whose deposit data (below: 228 bytes and the record padded to whole words) fits the portal's cap of 120,000 bytes, 32 bytes below `MAX_RELAY_RECORD` for the root's word (T-52).
3. **Header** (`relay-truncated`, `relay-record-type`): as `relayBallot`'s.

Its errors are `relayBallot`'s. The relay reads nothing else, the root included: whether the root is 0, a field element or the one the proof was made against is the board's to judge, which can verify the proof (Board, Relayed rolling ballots), and whether it is the root of the step the ballot names is the verifier's (`root-step-mismatch`). Then, with `i`, `b` and `τ` as for `relayBallot`, from the same count, it:

1. stores call `i`'s **call hash**

   ```
   call_hash = H(DS("abolish/v1/relay-rolling-call",
                    u64(l1_chain_id) ‖ bytes[20](relay) ‖ u64(i) ‖ u64(b) ‖ u64(τ) ‖
                    u64(gasLimit) ‖ bytes[32](root) ‖ record))
   ```

   and sets the count to `i + 1`;

2. emits `BallotRelayed` as `relayBallot` does, with this call hash;

3. calls `portal.depositTransaction(board, 0, gasLimit, false, data)` with

   ```
   data = abi.encodeCall(submitRelayedRollingBallot, (i, b, τ, gasLimit, root, record))
   ```

   the selector of `submitRelayedRollingBallot(uint64,uint64,uint64,uint64,bytes32,bytes)`, the words of `i`, `b`, `τ` and `gasLimit`, the root's word, the offset `0xc0`, the record's length, the record and its zero padding: `228 + pad32(len(record))` bytes;

4. returns `i`.

Everything Relaying says of the writes' order, a reverting deposit, the alias, the gas limit and emitting for every accepted deposit holds for this call too.

**Why a tag of its own.** Both calls write one word per index from one count, so a verifier knows only from the event which call wrote a word. Under one tag a call hash would need to say which call it is, or a rolling call's preimage would be a first call's whose record is the root's word followed by the ballot, an event of one kind matching a word of the other: `abolish/v1/relay-rolling-call` keeps every rolling call's word from matching any `RelayedBallotSubmitted` and every first call's from matching any `RelayedRollingBallotSubmitted` (T-31, T-71).

**Why the root in the hash.** The proof fixes the root (Board, Rolling ballots, One root per record), so a deposit with another root fails the board's check anyway; binding the root as well means a deposit L2 governance forges from the alias with the caller's record and another root, which the board's check would never reach, matches no call either, so the root the verifier compares with the step's is the caller's own (T-71).

**One count.** Indices, words and `BallotRelayed` events are shared by both calls: the count, `R + 1`'s mapping and the accumulator don't change, and a rolling call's index is the next after whatever call came before. A rolling ballot is no larger than a version-4 one but for its 4-byte step ([[ballot-validity]], Ballot record (version 5)), so every ballot record this spec defines fits `MAX_ROLLING_RELAY_RECORD` (Size, above).

### State

The values sit at the [ERC-7201](https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-7201) namespace `abolish.ballot-relay`:

```
R = keccak256(abi.encode(uint256(keccak256("abolish.ballot-relay")) − 1)) & ~0xff
  = 0xeddbfcaa40c756990a5dda7c565d5921d82a1a88a4b83bd8b0415aeae8667900
```

- the count of recorded calls is the word at `R`, a big-endian integer whose top 192 bits are zero;
- call `i`'s call hash is the word at `keccak256(abi.encode(uint256(i), R + 1))` (Solidity's `mapping(uint64 => bytes32)` at `R + 1`); zero means no call `i`.

Nothing else writes either, and neither is ever cleared: call `i`'s word is written once, by call `i`, whichever of the two calls it is (Rolling ballots, One count). The accumulator sits at `S` and `S + 1`, as every contract's (Event accumulator, Rules for every contract).

### Verification

For each relayed ballot on the board, the board's event names the call's index `i`, block `b`, stamp `τ` and gas limit and carries the record (`RelayedBallotSubmitted`, Board, Events). This departs from [[0030-close-rule]]'s wording in three ways, each stated here: the call binds the whole record, not the ballot hash (above); a word per call, not the accumulator alone, is what an event is matched against (above); and the stamp is the relay's own `block.timestamp`, written by the release code in the block it ran in, so the match needs no L1 header, though a verifier that reads header `b` for other checks also requires its timestamp to equal `τ` (`relay-unmatched` otherwise), defence in depth against storage the release code didn't write. A verifier proves the count word and call `i`'s word with `eth_getProof` against the state root of its finalized L1 block `B`, the block it proves anchors at (Board anchor, Verification), never from an `eth_call` answer, and the event **matches** call `i` when the word equals `call_hash` recomputed from the definition's `l1_chain_id` and `l1_relay` and the event's `i`, `b`, `τ`, gas limit and record: under `abolish/v1/relay-call` for a `RelayedBallotSubmitted`, and for a `RelayedRollingBallotSubmitted` under `abolish/v1/relay-rolling-call`, with the event's `root` as a word after the gas limit (Rolling ballots). It also requires `i` below the count and `b ≤ number(B)`, which the release code makes true of every word it writes: defence in depth, not what soundness rests on. It never reads the relay's logs or an L1 receipt for this, so the match survives L1 history expiry ([EIP-4444](https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-4444)), as the anchors do.

- **`relay-unmatched`**: an event that matches no call fails [[verifier]] 4.5. Only L2 governance (or a false output root) can make one, since only a deposit from the relay's alias reaches the board's relayed-ballot call, and every such deposit was made by a recorded call with exactly these fields (T-71).
- **`relay-duplicate`**: two events that name one index, of either event or one of each, fail 4.5 too. A word holds one hash, so at most one record matches it, and each call made one deposit, which runs once on L2 and emits once (Board, Relayed ballots), so two events for one index are a forgery, whichever matches.

The stamp of a matched event is then the timestamp of the L1 block the call ran in, written by the relay itself, which no sequencer or caller chooses; it is what [[close]] (Window) judges. The match rests on the release relay code (1.2): its constructor's storage isn't bound yet, like every contract's (Board anchor, Known-weak, Constructor effects aren't bound), so until it is, a deployer's initcode could set the count and pre-write words below it that a forged deposit would then match. Proving the count zero at the deployment's own block, where an archive proof exists, narrows that, and is left to the binding.

**Calls with no event**, reported, never failed: a call whose deposit reverted on L2 (too little gas, a record the board refuses, a stamp outside the window) or that the L2 hasn't yet included leaves a word with no event. A byte-identical repeat never does: the board emits for every relayed deposit it accepts (Relaying). While L1 history serves the relay's logs, a verifier reads the `BallotRelayed` logs naming the election's definition hash, checked against the relay's accumulator (Event accumulator, Verification), and reports each call no board event matches, with its stamp, as [[0030-close-rule]] (Consequences, The verifier) requires; once the logs have expired, only the count and the words remain, which don't say which election a call was for. Storing each call's definition hash too would keep that for good, at a second new storage word per call (about 20,000 gas); it isn't, since the report is never a verdict and an election's own relayed ballots are all matched from the board. A call outside the window is refused by the board and counts nothing either way.

### Vectors

`vectors/relay.json` (format `abolish-relay-vectors/1`, see [[vectors/README|vectors]]) gives the selectors and the event's topic 0, the storage slots, L2 aliases (one wrapping modulo 2¹⁶⁰), calls with their call hashes, deposit data, topics and event data (the shortest record, a record relayed twice, another relay, another L1, the `u64` extremes, about the largest version-4 record and a record of `MAX_RELAY_RECORD` bytes), the framing of records at and around each bound and each refused type, canonical and non-canonical calldata, and the relay's event accumulator over three calls. `packages/crypto` consumes it, and so does `test/BallotRelay.t.sol`: each call is made at its own relay address, L1 chain, index, block and stamp, and its returned index, stored word, count, storage slots, event and deposit (target, value, gas limit and data) are the file's; every framing and calldata verdict holds (a calldata fault Solidity's decoder finds first, a word above 2⁶⁴, a length past the end or another selector, reverts with no data); the accumulator over three calls is the file's; and call `2⁶⁴ − 1`, whose hash the file gives, is never recorded, since the count would leave a `u64`. Further tests: a refused deposit records nothing, a call re-entering through the portal records the next call after this one, the deposit data is the board's canonical call for any gas limit and record, and exactly the records with a ballot header are recorded. The verifier's 4.5 will consume it too (P1-19).

`vectors/relay-rolling.json` (format `abolish-relay-rolling-vectors/1`) gives the rolling call (Rolling ballots), from an independent generator over `relay.json`'s: its selectors, calls with their call hashes, deposit data, topics and event data (a version-5 ballot of `ballot-v5-records.json` with the root of the step it names, the same call with another step's root, the same record and root relayed again, the roots 0, `r` and `2²⁵⁶ − 1`, which the relay records and the board refuses, another relay, another L1, the `u64` extremes and a record of `MAX_ROLLING_RELAY_RECORD` bytes), each beside the word `relayBallot` would store for the same fields (`fixedCallHash`, never the same), the framing of records at and around both bounds, canonical and non-canonical calldata (the root's word any value, `relayBallot`'s layout under this selector and the reverse), and one relay's sequence of both calls (an index, block, stamp, word, slot, event and accumulator per call). `packages/crypto` runs it through the call's helpers (framing, call hash, deposit data and calldata) since [[P2-17]] part 3d; `test/BallotRelay.t.sol` is to run every call, framing and calldata vector through the rolling call, and the sequence through one relay, in part 5.

### Known-weak

- **Anyone can record calls.** The relay frames only the header, so a call with a record the board refuses, or too little gas, is recorded and deposited and produces no event: each costs its caller L1 gas and leaves a word and a report, never a failure. Its whole record (up to `MAX_RELAY_RECORD` bytes of the caller's choice) is in the caller's L1 calldata, in the portal's `TransactionDeposited` log on L1 and in the deposit transaction on L2, from the relay's alias to the board, for good, as a reverting direct transaction to the board would be. It never reaches the board or our archive, since the board refuses it (T-17).
- **The portal is the L2's.** L2 governance can upgrade the portal or change how deposits are derived, which can censor forced ballots (T-50, T-71); it can also forge a deposit from the relay's alias, which 4.5 catches (`relay-unmatched`). A portal upgraded to refuse deposits makes every relay call revert, so nothing is recorded that wasn't deposited.
- **The stamp is the L1 slot's start.** A proposer can include a call made a few seconds after `closes_at` in a block stamped at or before it ([[close]], Residuals, The relay's seconds; T-35, T-45).
- **Unmatched calls are reported only while L1 serves the relay's logs** (above); the words and the count stay in state for good.
- **A rolling call's root is the caller's.** The relay records whatever root word the caller gives, so a call can leave 32 chosen bytes in L1 state's preimage and calldata, whatever the board does with its deposit (T-17), as the record's own bytes can; a deposit the board refuses archives nothing on L2 (Board, Relayed rolling ballots).
- **The verifier doesn't match calls yet.** Verifier 4.5 is P1-19's; until it is built, a relayed ballot's stamp is the event's word, so the verifier sets a relayed ballot aside after 5.1 to 5.4 and leaves 5.5, 5.7 and everything after admission unverifiable, never judging it by an unproven stamp ([[verifier]], 5.5; T-71).

## Release registry

Threats: T-41 (a client served to one voter), T-69 (a compelled or seized release key), T-64 (a deployment that isn't the release code), T-17 (chosen bytes on L1), T-36 (two verifiers reading one release two ways).

The release registry is our third contract on L1, decided by [[0036-release-record]] (option B). It holds the release hash of every ballot-client release ([[release]], Release hash) in one chain, numbered from 0, and a revocation for any of them, so that a release is public on L1 before any check passes it, and [[verifier]] 1.1 and `apps/node` prove a release published and not revoked from a finalized L1 header with the storage proofs the verifier already reads for anchors. It is immutable: no owner, proxy, pause or upgrade path. Its one writer is fixed at deployment as an immutable. A verifier release pins its chain ID, address, authority and runtime code hash ([[verifier]], Pinned trust roots): the authority is its only immutable, so the release build with the pinned authority written in is its whole runtime code, and a client run compares the proven account's code hash with the pin ([[verifier]], Served client, step 2). A release record names the registry it is appended to (`l1_chain_id`, `registry`), so one record counts in one registry only.

The registry never sees a record. A record is up to about 2.6 MB ([[release]], Decoding), more than it is worth decoding on L1, so the registry stores only the hash it is given, and everything that ties the hash to a record (that the record decodes and is well formed, that its `sequence`, `previous`, `registry` and `l1_chain_id` agree with the registry, that its files make its `directory`) is checked by the release Safe's signers before they sign ([[P4-4]] part 2e) and by 1.1 after ([[verifier]], Served client). A hash the Safe appends without its record, or with a record that breaks any of that, fails 1.1 for that release, publicly; it can't make a client pass.

### Deployment

`constructor(address authority)` stores `authority` as an immutable. Deployment reverts if `authority` has no code or only an [EIP-7702](https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-7702) delegation (`0xef0100 ‖ address`), as the anchor's does (Board anchor, Deployment): both are a single key. The authority is the **release Safe** of [[0020-entry-points]], the Safe that also holds `abolishus.eth`, a SafeL2 used with no module, guard or delegatecall, for the reasons the anchor's Known-weak gives; the deployment record (`packages/contracts/deployments/<chain-id>.json`) names it, its owners and its threshold. Until the owner deploys a registry on mainnet and names the Safe's signers, the registry is Sepolia's ([[0036-release-record]], Consequences).

### Calls

The registry has two calls, `publish` and `revoke`, both non-payable. Any other calldata, empty calldata included, reverts: the contract has no `receive`, no `fallback` and no getter (state is read by proof, State below), so nothing else can change it or be mistaken for it. A call carrying value reverts.

### Publishing

`publish(bytes32 releaseHash, bytes32 previous)` appends a release. It reverts, changing nothing, unless:

1. the caller is `authority` (T-41: no one else can make a release count);
2. the calldata is exactly the selector and the two words, 68 bytes, nothing after (T-17);
3. `releaseHash` isn't 32 zero bytes, so a zero word always means no release;
4. `previous` is the release hash at sequence `count − 1`, or 32 zero bytes when `count` is 0.

Rule 4 makes the registry a chain with no forks: a Safe transaction built against a head that has since moved reverts, so two releases signed in parallel can't both take one sequence, and a signer approving a release approves its place after the release it names. The call then stores `releaseHash` and `published_at = block.timestamp` at sequence `s = count`, sets `count` to `s + 1`, and emits, through the event accumulator (above), with `chain_id` the L1's,

| Event                                                                                      | Topics                               | Data                    |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------ | ----------------------- |
| `ReleasePublished(uint64 indexed sequence, bytes32 indexed releaseHash, bytes32 previous)` | `s` as a 32-byte word, `releaseHash` | `previous`, ABI-encoded |

`previous` repeats what the record itself binds, so the record's `previous` is the hash at `s − 1` exactly when the release Safe built the record for that place; 1.1 checks the record's, never the call's.

The registry doesn't refuse a hash it already holds at another sequence: a well-formed record binds its own `sequence`, so its hash can count at that one place only, and 1.1 reads the record at the sequence it names. A second copy is the Safe's public mistake and judges nothing.

### Revocation

`revoke(uint64 sequence)` revokes a published release. It reverts, changing nothing, unless:

1. the caller is `authority`;
2. the calldata is exactly the selector and one word holding `sequence` with its high 192 bits zero, 36 bytes, nothing after (T-17);
3. `sequence` is below `count`;
4. the release at `sequence` isn't already revoked.

It then sets that release's `revoked_at` to `block.timestamp` and emits

| Event                                                                  | Topics                                 | Data |
| ---------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------- | ---- |
| `ReleaseRevoked(uint64 indexed sequence, bytes32 indexed releaseHash)` | `sequence` as a 32-byte word, its hash | none |

Nothing clears `revoked_at`, so a revocation is final, and nothing else writes a release's hash or `published_at`, so a release, once published, stays at its place for good (T-69). Revoking a release doesn't move the chain: the next release still names the latest hash as its `previous`, revoked or not, and a revoked latest release stays the latest until another is published. The Safe revokes a release that should never have run (a leaked signing session, a build that isn't its commit's, a client with a flaw that loses votes) and publishes its fix as the next release. A revocation says nothing about why; the Safe's signers say that in public, outside the chain.

### State

The registry keeps its state at fixed slots, so a verifier reads it from a storage proof alone and it survives L1 history expiry ([EIP-4444](https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-4444)), as the anchor's does. The base is the [ERC-7201](https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-7201) namespace `abolish.release-registry`:

```
R = keccak256(abi.encode(uint256(keccak256("abolish.release-registry")) − 1)) & ~0xff
  = 0x70e6df55cd9db89509d405697815053719fd2c44a78b3358c3b14b5923208800
```

- `count`, the number of releases published, is the word at `R`, a `uint64`: its high 192 bits are zero.
- Release `s`'s hash is the word at `keccak256(abi.encode(uint256(s), R + 1))`, and its times word is the next slot: `published_at` in its low 64 bits, `revoked_at` in the next 64, the rest zero (Solidity's `mapping(uint64 => Release)` at `R + 1`, with `struct Release { bytes32 releaseHash; uint64 publishedAt; uint64 revokedAt; }`). `revoked_at` is zero for a release never revoked.

The release code writes only words that meet these rules, at a block `B` with timestamp `now`: `count` below 2⁶⁴; for each `s` below `count`, a non-zero hash, and a times word with its high 128 bits zero, `published_at` from 1 to `now`, and `revoked_at` zero or from `published_at` to `now`; and nothing at a sequence from `count` up. A word that breaks them can only come from code that isn't the release's, or from a constructor's own writes (Board anchor, Known-weak, Constructor effects aren't bound), and 1.1 fails on it (`release-registry-state`) rather than read a release from it.

### Verification

A verifier reads the registry from state only, never from `ReleasePublished` logs, which an RPC node can leave out, nor from an `eth_call` answer. It proves the registry's account, `count`, and the hash and times words of the sequences it judges with `eth_getProof` at one finalized L1 block `B`, walking the trie as Anchors from L1 does ([[verifier]], steps 1 to 3, with the registry's address in place of the anchor's), and `B`'s timestamp is the `now` of the rules above. Because no release is ever removed, the release at sequence `s` at `B` is the release at `s` at every later block; only `count` and the revocations grow. So a release proven published at `B` was public on L1 from its `published_at` on, to anyone reading the registry, which is the property T-41 needs: a release built for one voter is a release everyone can see.

The events are for watching, not for proof: a monitoring job alerts on every `ReleasePublished` and `ReleaseRevoked` (P2-11), and `apps/node` can follow the chain from them while L1 history serves its logs, checking what it finds against the registry's accumulator (Event accumulator, Verification) or the words.

### Vectors

`vectors/release-registry.json` (format `abolish-release-registry-vectors/1`, see [[vectors/README|vectors]]) gives the namespace and slots (sequences 0 to 2 and 2⁶⁴ − 1), the two selectors and the two events' topic 0, a history of calls against the rules above (each refused call with the rule it breaks, each accepted one with its log) with the words it leaves, and 1.1's judgments over proven words ([[verifier]], Served client). `packages/crypto/tests/release-registry-vectors.test.ts` checks the slots, selectors, topics and the history's logs and words with `@noble/hashes`' Keccak-256; the registry's Foundry tests (part 2d) replay the history and compare every word.

### Known-weak

- **The Safe can publish a bad release**, or be compelled to: a record with a weaker CSP or a client that leaks votes counts once its hash is appended. The registry makes it public at once, not impossible (T-41, T-69), and the monitoring job is what turns public into seen.
- **The Safe can revoke a good release**, which makes 1.1 fail for every client of it until a new one is published: a liveness attack on the client-integrity verdict, attributable on L1, never on an election's result (1.1 is report-only).
- **The Safe's configuration can change after deployment**, as for the anchor (Board anchor, Known-weak): the registry pins its address only, and the Safe's own history is the check, which the release runbook (part 2e) applies before every signature and the verifier doesn't yet.
- **The record isn't on L1.** The registry holds its hash, and the record is published to IPFS and the archive beside each release (part 2e). A record no one can produce leaves 1.1 unverifiable for that release (`release-record-missing`), never passed: the hash authenticates whatever copy turns up, from any source.
- **Constructor effects aren't bound** (Board anchor, Known-weak): the code-hash pin proves the runtime code, not what the constructor wrote, so until deployments are bound to the release creation code, a deployer's initcode could write releases the authority never published. 1.1 reads them as published, so a passing 1.1 is as authentic as the deployment until that binding exists.
- **Gas**, estimated until part 2d measures it: about 100,000 per publication (two new words, the count, the accumulator, a log and the transaction's base cost) and 45,000 per revocation, on L1, once per release: free on Sepolia; on mainnet a payment the owner makes.
- **The registry isn't deployed or built yet**: its contract and tests are [[P4-4]] part 2d, its deployment a `ci:` PR after it, and the release workflow that publishes to it part 2e. Until then no release is published, and 1.1 is unverifiable with `release-none` on every client.
